1.3 DC-specific federal control — the Harris rider, congressional review, historical blocks

Current rider — exact text (as carried in FY2026 FSGG bills and quoted verbatim in bill coverage; enrolled P.L. 119-75 section number VERIFY as in Part 0):

“SEC. 809. (a) None of the Federal funds contained in this Act may be used to enact or carry out any law, rule, or regulation to legalize or otherwise reduce penalties associated with the possession, use, or distribution of any schedule I substance under the Controlled Substances Act (21 U.S.C. 801 et seq.) or any tetrahydrocannabinols derivative.

(b) No funds available for obligation or expenditure by the District of Columbia government under any authority may be used to enact any law, rule, or regulation to legalize or otherwise reduce penalties associated with the possession, use, or distribution of any schedule I substance under the Controlled Substances Act (21 U.S.C. 801 et seq.) or any tetrahydrocannabinols derivative for recreational purposes.”

— Quoted in Marijuana Moment (House passage) and Marijuana Moment (signing)

Public law and duration. FY2026 — Consolidated Appropriations Act, 2026, P.L. 119-75, signed 3 February 2026 (H.R. 7148; House vote 217–214 on Senate amendments, Senate 71–29 per the Senate Appropriations release). The rider is an annual appropriations provision: it lasts only for the fiscal year / funding period it is enacted for (FY2026 = year ending 30 September 2026, as extended by any continuing resolution in force). It must be re-enacted each year to continue; it is not permanent law. First enacted for FY2015 (following Initiative 71's November 2014 passage) and renewed every year since — the “since FY2015” continuity is consistently reported; the original FY2015 public law number was not re-verified in this run VERIFY. Sources: Senate Appropriations release; CRS status table confirming P.L. 119-75 (PDF).

Exactly what it does and does not block:

  • Does block: DC government spending (federal funds and any District funds “under any authority” — subsection (b) is deliberately broader than subsection (a)) to enact any law/rule/regulation legalising or reducing penalties for recreational Schedule I / tetrahydrocannabinols-derivative possession, use or distribution. In practice: no DC-funded adult-use licensing, regulation, or taxation system; no DC-funded rulemaking to commercialise adult-use sales. Subsection (a) additionally blocks federal funds to carry out such a law.
  • Does not block: (i) Initiative 71 itself — the rider “did not retroactively repeal the initiative, instead leaving it in place while prohibiting further legalization” (CRS, LSB11188); (ii) the medical programme — medical cannabis is not “for recreational purposes” under (b), and DC funds the ABCA medical programme openly; (iii) DC enacting a law with no expenditure (theoretical — in practice licensing/regulation/taxation all require expenditure, which is why no adult-use bill can be implemented while the rider stands); (iv) private activity that needs no District funds (e.g. private gifting within §48-904.01 limits — though commercial “gifting” shops are separately unlawful under Chapter 16B, see §7-1671.08); (v) Congress itself legalising DC adult-use sales by federal law.
  • CRS rescheduling interaction (LSB11188, 2024 — pre-dating the split Schedule III rule, so read with the baseline): CRS said rescheduling marijuana to Schedule III would permit DC to (1) repeal, in whole or part, local prohibitions on recreational possession/use beyond Initiative 71, and (2) repeal, in whole or part, local prohibitions on distribution including commercial recreational sale — because the rider as written reaches only Schedule I substances. Under the actual 2026 split rule, that logic reaches only the Schedule III portion (FDA-approved products and state-licensed medical marijuana); adult-use marijuana remains Schedule I, so subsection (b) still blocks DC from enacting a recreational-sales law for adult-use marijuana. The rider's additional phrase “or any tetrahydrocannabinols derivative” is the textual uncertainty CRS/advocates flag: a court or Congress would have to decide whether a THC derivative in a Schedule III medical product is still caught. VERIFY — no court ruling or DC official interpretation on this point was found. CRS LSB11188 (PDF)

FY2027 status VERIFY: Secondary reporting says the House FY2027 FSGG bill (released April 2026) again carries the ban, and a stopgap runs to 11 December 2026. The ACLU-DC August 2026 one-pager on the FY27 FSGG bill, H.R. 8495, lists problem riders at §§823, 824, 827, 831, 833 — the cannabis rider's section in H.R. 8495 was not visible in the pages opened, so FY2027 rider presence/section is not confirmed from a primary source in this run and must not be stated as enacted. ACLU-DC one-pager (PDF) No federal cannabis banking law has been enacted (baseline; recurring FSGG “no penalty for serving state-legal businesses” language in some House bills was appropriations language, not a standalone banking safe harbour, and is not law).

Congressional review process (Home Rule Act §602(c), D.C. Code §1-206.02(c)):

  • Council acts (and initiative/referendum enactments) must be transmitted by the Council Chairman to the Speaker of the House and the President of the Senate.
  • Layover: 30 calendar days for ordinary laws; 60 calendar days for laws codified in DC Code Titles 22 (Criminal Offenses), 23 (Criminal Procedure) or 24 (Prisoners) — counting excludes Saturdays, Sundays, federal holidays, and days neither House is in session due to adjournment sine die or an adjournment resolution.
  • The law takes effect on expiry of the layover (or a later date in the act) unless Congress enacts a joint resolution of disapproval (with special fast-track Senate procedures) and it is signed (or a veto overridden).
  • Emergency acts (§412(a), §1-204.12(a)) take effect immediately on Mayoral approval but last no longer than 90 days; congressional-review emergency acts are a separate device used to bridge review.

CRS summary: CRS IN12119. House Report 119-463 (proposed Home Rule Improvement Act of 2025, not enacted — would change 30→60 days and remove the criminal-law distinction) — congress.gov — do not present its proposals as current law.

Cannabis laws and review — outcomes found. Initiative 71: transmitted, review period lapsed without disapproval, effective 26 Feb 2015 (OAG FAQ). Medical Cannabis Amendment Act of 2022 and subsequent medical acts: in force as codified (no disapproval identified). Act 26-391 (2026, emergency): in force for ≤90 days (to 28 Oct 2026 per Law Library emergency notes); its permanent/temporary successors were not identified in this run VERIFY. No cannabis-specific joint resolution of disapproval was identified as enacted in sources opened in this run.

Historical congressional blocks:

  • Initiative 59 / Barr Amendment (1998–2009). DC voters approved the Legalization of Marijuana for Medical Treatment Initiative of 1998 (Initiative 59) in November 1998 with 69% of the vote (revealed only after ACLU litigation, because the first Barr Amendment also barred release of the tally). Rep. Bob Barr's amendments to the FY1999 and FY2000 omnibus appropriations bills barred DC from spending funds to implement Initiative 59 (the FY2000 version overturned it outright) and barred future laws decreasing penalties for marijuana/Schedule I drugs. A March 2002 district-court ruling striking the speech-restrictive portion was reversed on appeal, reinstating the bar in time to thwart Initiative 63 (2002, already ballot-qualified). In 2009 both Houses voted to lift the ban, effectively overturning the Barr Amendment; the first lawful DC medical sale followed in July 2013 (Capital City Care). Sources are secondary — the underlying public-law citations for the Barr Amendments were not re-verified on congress.gov/GovInfo in this run VERIFY. Initiative 59 (Wikipedia); NORML, first sale
  • Harris rider (2014/2015–present). Added in December 2014 after Initiative 71 passed (November 2014) — it did not stop Initiative 71 taking effect in February 2015, but has blocked every subsequent attempt to fund an adult-use sales system.
  • 2014 decriminalisation context: the Marijuana Possession Decriminalization Amendment Act of 2014 (effective 17 July 2014) replaced the criminal penalty for ≤1 oz possession with a $25 civil fine, preceding Initiative 71 — secondary summary via MPP; official act number VERIFY. MPP overview

1.4 Possession limits, home grow, public consumption, gifting, product types

ItemRule (official)Source
Adult possession (non-medical)≤2 oz marijuana — possess, use, purchase, transport (21+)§48-904.01(a)(1)(A)
Gifting (non-medical)≤1 oz, to another 21+, without remuneration only. Any sale / exchange for money, goods or services is unlawful; commercial “gifting” (buy a sticker/art, get cannabis) is not protected — it is unlicensed commercial activity under Chapter 16B§48-904.01(a)(1)(B); §7-1671.01(22), §7-1671.08
Home grow≤6 plants/person, ≤3 mature/flowering, inside principal residence only; household aggregate ≤12/≤6; may possess the marijuana those plants produce inside that residence; may NOT sell/offer/make available for sale§48-904.01(a)(1)(C)–(D)
Public consumptionProhibited: public space; street/alley/park/sidewalk/parking area; vehicle on any of those; any place to which the public is invited. Exception: licensed safe-use treatment facility / summer garden (medical patients only, on-premises, designated area)OAG FAQ; §§7-1671.03(b)(4), 7-1671.06c, 7-1671.06f
Medical possession≤8 oz dried medical cannabis at any moment (patient or caregiver); the Mayor by rulemaking sets limits for non-dried forms§7-1671.03(a)
Medical dispensing≤8 oz per 30-day period per patient (directly or through a caregiver); the Mayor may by rulemaking set alternate limits for non-dried forms§7-1671.06(o)
Medical product types soldPer ABCA's Aug 2026 retailer sales-by-type: flower/bud, concentrate, infused concentrate, infused edible, infused pre-roll, infused topical, kief, pill/capsule/suppository, raw pre-roll, shake/trim, tincture, vape cartridge. Beverages are not separately distinguished in the report, and no per-serving or per-package THC mg limit was found in the Code sections opened. §7-1671.06(o)(2) expressly reserves non-dried-form limits to Mayoral rulemaking. Any “10 mg/serving, 100 mg/package” figure for DC is VERIFY — that is another jurisdiction's standard and was NOT found in DC law in this run; do not import it.ABCA Aug 2026 report; §7-1671.06(o)
ParaphernaliaLawful for 21+ in connection with lawful use (Initiative 71 ballot summary); medical paraphernalia regulated under Chapter 16B; no medical cannabis/paraphernalia at a licensed facility may be visible from public/other property§48-904.01; §7-1671.06(p)
DrivingDriving under the influence / impaired driving remains criminal; §48-904.01(a)(1D) preserves DUI law; §7-1671.03(d)(2) bars operating/navigating/actual physical control of a motor vehicle, aircraft or motorboat under the influence of medical cannabis§§48-904.01(a)(1D), 7-1671.03(d)

1.5 Medical programme — detail

  • Qualifying conditions: no list. “Any condition for which treatment with medical cannabis would be beneficial, as determined by an authorized practitioner” (§7-1671.01(17)); qualifying treatment includes chemotherapy, AZT/protease inhibitors, radiotherapy, or any other treatment whose side effects require medical-cannabis treatment as determined by rulemaking (§7-1671.01(18)).
  • Self-certification (21+): the form created under §7-1671.05(b)(3) carries this verbatim statement: “I will only use cannabis purchased from a District retailer as a qualifying patient for the treatment of a qualifying medical or dental condition or for the side effects of a qualifying or medical treatment. I understand my rights and obligations as set forth by the Medical Cannabis Program and agree to these requirements. I certify under penalty of perjury that the foregoing is true and correct.” A false material statement carries a fine ≤$1,000 and/or ≤180 days (§22-2405, as cited in the form).
  • How to register (ABCA current page): all registrations via the ABCA Customer Portal (now MyABCA.DC.gov, launched 18 Aug 2026 — legacy registrations submitted before 8:00 a.m. 18 Aug 2026 still process in the legacy system and do not transfer). DC resident: temporary digital registration valid 90 days on submission, usable immediately; digital registration valid 2 years after staff review; free; government photo ID + proof of DC residency (DMV Real ID, or ID + utility bill / bank-credit statement / mortgage / rental / government mail). Ages 18–20: ABCA's page says a recommendation number is required “if between 18-20 years old or not self-certifying”; the statute requires a practitioner recommendation for under-21s generally, with minors also needing a caregiver + parental statement — read the ABCA page and statute together. Discounted (sliding-scale) product: Medicaid/DC Alliance card or 2 proofs of household income ≤200% of the federal poverty level + pay stubs/tax returns/etc.; permanent registration only, not temporary.
  • Non-residents: 21+ only; self-certification required (ABCA table: “recommendations are not accepted” for non-residents); no caregiver; digital only; fee $10–$100 by selected duration; ABCA's table lists valid periods of 30/90/180/365 days (the table omits the statutory 3-day minimum — statute: “between 3 days and no longer than one year,” §7-1671.05(b)(4)) — which durations are actually offered in the portal today is VERIFY. Renewal within 30 days of expiry keeps the number; outside that window it is a new registration.
  • Caregivers: ≥18, designated by the patient, authorised to possess/obtain from a licensed retailer/internet retailer/courier, dispense, administer and assist in administration (§7-1671.01(3)); no criminal background check on caregiver registration (§7-1671.02(b-1)); must notify ABCA in writing within 48 hours of loss/theft/destruction of a registration card (§7-1671.05(b)(19)). August 2026: only 32 unique caregivers registered (ABCA report) — a strikingly low figure vs 143,556 patients, suggesting most patients self-serve; reported as published, not treated as a data error.
  • Out-of-state / visitor rules — two distinct routes: (1) Reciprocity: licensed DC retailers “are permitted to accept patient registrations from most U.S. states, territories, and select tribal nations” (ABCA) — the patient must present a home registration card/digital credential + government photo ID; ABCA publishes a full eligible-jurisdictions list (linked from the Patients page). An ABCA June 2025-era release noted non-resident patients from 40 jurisdictions could purchase via reciprocity. (2) Temporary DC registration: the non-resident cardholder route above (self-certified, fee, 3 days–1 year). ABCA may suspend reciprocity/temporary access if it determines a shortage or the real-time records system is inactive (§7-1671.01(19) proviso).
  • What federal Schedule III means for the DC programme — DC official position: UNAVAILABLE. No ABCA/Council/OTR statement was found saying DC-licensed medical businesses are, or are not, within the 91 FR 22714 “state-licensed medical” carve-out, nor on DC tax treatment after rescheduling. Secondary analysis flags the “DC is not a state; ABCA is a District agency” question as unresolved. Practical consequences if the carve-out does reach DC medical: federal §280E falls away for qualifying DC medical activity (Treasury guidance forthcoming per baseline), and the DC §280E proviso becomes redundant for that activity. If it does not reach DC medical: federal §280E continues for DC medical businesses, while the DC proviso (if applicable — Part 2) still shelters District income tax only. Do not advise either way; this needs a DC official statement or tax counsel.
  • First sales / programme history SECONDARY ONLY: first lawful medical sale July 2013 (Capital City Care) — NORML summary. ABCA took over the programme from the Department of Health on 1 October 2020 (Medical Marijuana Program Administration Amendment Act of 2020, D.C. Law 23-149, effective 3 Dec 2020). First testing laboratory licensed 16 August 2024 (ClearSight Labs), triggering mandatory testing — ABCA notice.

1.6 Hemp

  • DC law has no hemp definition, no hemp THC limit, no hemp cultivation programme, and no hemp-product licensing regime in the Code sections opened. §48-901.02(3) defines cannabis/marijuana/hashish without a hemp carve-out (mature stalks/fibre/seed oil/sterilised seed only). Therefore, on DC's own definitions, a cannabinoid product derived from cannabis is “cannabis” regardless of its federal hemp status — that is the OAG AL-21-222 position and ABCA's enforcement position.
  • Delta-8 / intoxicating hemp: treated as controlled/cannabis under DC law on that reasoning; ABCA has shut/settled hemp retailers on that basis (District Hemp Offer in Compromise, Apr 2026; Capitol Hemp / District Hemp DLCP infractions June 2024, summarised in respondent correspondence). Whether §48-902.04 expressly names delta-8 THC was not confirmed on the official section text VERIFY — do not assert it.
  • THC measurement in DC: no DC total-THC (THCA-inclusive) measurement rule was found in Chapter 16B or the Title 22-C excerpts opened. Testing labs must report THC and CBD concentrations (§7-1671.05(b)(9)/(11)), and ABCA investigators may test to “quantify THC content” (§7-1671.08(i)(1)), but the DC conversion formula (if any) is VERIFY.
  • THC drinks: no DC law authorising general hemp-THC drinks was found. Medical cannabis beverages exist only as a pending proposal (B26-0654, §1.10) — under current law a “THC drink” sold outside the medical programme is an unlicensed cannabis product; inside the programme it would be a medical cannabis product subject to testing/labelling/dispensing rules (beverage as a product form is not separately regulated in the Code sections opened — VERIFY in 22-C).
  • DC official response to the new federal hemp definition: UNAVAILABLE. No ABCA, Council, OAG or Mayor statement was found addressing P.L. 119-37 §781 or P.L. 119-103 §2019. Applying the baseline dates mechanically: until 11 Dec 2026 only the “not naturally produced / synthesized” exclusion provisions are limited in effect; the full total-THC definition + 0.4 mg/container cap applies after 11 Dec 2026. Because DC law already treats hemp-derived cannabinoids as cannabis, the federal change's practical DC effect is likely smaller than in states with open hemp markets — but that is analysis, not a DC official statement, and is labelled as such. Do not state the full federal definition takes effect 12 Nov 2026.
  • USDA DEA-lab testing delay to 31 Dec 2026 (baseline): no DC implementation statement found UNAVAILABLE — DC has no hemp cultivation programme for USDA rules to attach to in any event.

1.7 Packaging, labelling, advertising, delivery/courier, zoning/buffers

  • Packaging/labelling (statute): child-resistant per 16 C.F.R. Part 1700; no overconsumption promotion, no under-21 consumption depiction, no child-appealing content (§1.2E). Medical cannabis/products at a facility must not be visible from public/other property (§7-1671.06(p)). Transport in a labelled container/sealed package (§7-1671.03(c)). Testing/labelling detail lives in 22-C Chs. 56, 64–66 — section text VERIFY.
  • Advertising: Chapter 22-C58 exists; detailed prohibitions VERIFY (not opened). Statutory advertising-adjacent rules: no Initiative 71-compliance representation (§7-1671.06(c-1)); no price/product display on unlicensed-establishment windows/doors/exterior (§7-1671.06b(e)); no advertising of prohibited safe-use / education practices (§§7-1671.06c(d)(6), 7-1671.06d(c)(2)); unlicensed applicants may be cease-and-desisted for child-appealing packaging/ads (§7-1671.06b(a)).
  • Delivery/courier: see §7-1671.06e (Part 1 §1.2) and the courier licence in Part 4. Courier = platform/business licensed in DC, under contract with a retailer/internet retailer licensee, using own employees/independent contractors (§7-1671.01(4A)). A courier licence holder may hold NO retailer/internet/cultivation/lab/manufacturer licence (§7-1671.06(e)(1)(B)).
  • Zoning/buffers: see Part 3 §3.5 (400 ft / 300 ft grandfathering; 400 ft retailer-to-retailer; no residential district; daycare considerations).

1.8 Seed-to-sale tracking

Named by an official DC source: YES — METRC. ABCA's Medical Cannabis Business Licenses page links “METRC Business Training” (metrc.com), and ABCA rulemakings define: “METRC — means the electronic track and trace platform provided by the Marijuana Enforcement Tracking Reporting Compliance (METRC) company.” The Code requires “true, complete, and real-time electronic records” (§7-1671.05(b)(6)–(7)) and defines “real-time electronic records” as a system “able to track the amount of medical cannabis that District residents and patients from another jurisdiction purchase in real-time” (§7-1671.01(19A)). Whether every licensee must use Metrc as the designated system (vs. any compliant real-time system) is stated in rulemaking, not in the Code sections opened — the rulemaking mandate wording is VERIFY, but the system name itself is officially confirmed. Sources: ABCA Licenses page; 9th technical rulemaking (PDF).

1.9 Employment and drug testing protections

See §1.2D (D.C. Law 24-190). Summary: no adverse action based on (1) use of cannabis, (2) medical programme patient status, or (3) cannabinoid metabolites without additional impairment factors; exceptions for safety-sensitive designation, federal mandate, on-duty/workplace use/possession, and articulable impairment. Also note: §48-904.01(a)(1C) preserves employers' ability to restrict workplace use; the medical programme does not require employers to permit on-duty use except as HRA §211(b-1) / CMPA §2062 provide for medical patients (reasonable-accommodation-style treatment as legal use of a controlled substance under professional supervision — secondary summary; statutory text VERIFY). Pre-employment testing timing (only after a conditional offer) appears in secondary summaries of prior DC law — VERIFY before citing. Complaint/enforcement route and penalty figures in secondary summaries (Office of Human Rights; $1,000/$2,500/$5,000 tiers) were not confirmed on an official source in this run VERIFY.

Part 2 — Taxes

2.1 Medical cannabis sales tax — current law, and the dropped increase

Current law (DC Law Library, read directly):

§47-2002(a)(7)(A): “The rate of tax shall be 6% of the gross receipts from the sale of or charges for medical cannabis, as defined in [Chapter 16B of Title 7].”

§47-2002(a)(7)(B): “In Fiscal Year 2026, the proceeds of the tax collected under subparagraph (A) of this paragraph shall be deposited in the Healthy DC and Health Care Expansion Fund established by §31-3514.02; except, that all proceeds of such tax above the amount certified for Fiscal Year 2026 in the approved Fiscal Year 2023 budget shall be deposited in the General Fund of the District of Columbia.”

— D.C. Code §47-2002

Interaction with the general rate rise VERIFY: the §47-2002(a) lead-in reads: “The rate of such tax … shall be 6.0% before October 1, 2026, and 7.0% beginning on October 1, 2026, and continuing thereafter; except, that:” — followed by paragraph (7) fixing medical cannabis at 6%. On a plain reading the specific 6% medical rate survives the general 6%→7% rise, but OTR guidance confirming medical cannabis stays at 6% from 1 Oct 2026 was not opened in this run — verify with OTR before advising a retailer. No separate DC cannabis excise tax exists in the Code sections opened.

The 10.25% proposal — NOT law. The Mayor's FY2027 budget proposal (Medical Cannabis Tax Rate Amendment Act of 2026) would have raised the rate to 10.25% from 1 October 2026 (OCFO estimate cited secondarily: +$1.54m in FY2027, +$6.33m through FY2030). Industry reporting in June 2026 says the Council removed it from the final budget package (vote expected 9 June 2026). It does not appear in the current §47-2002 text read. Treat 10.25% as a dead proposal, not an upcoming change, unless a later enacted budget act is produced.

Tax holiday — 2024 only in current law. The enrolled 2022 act set the “4/20 Medical Cannabis Sales Tax Holiday Week” as 15–24 April each calendar year (with the 2024 emergency act extending 2024 to 15–28 April; 6% waived on medical cannabis products except paraphernalia, at licensed retailers only, for DC + non-DC patients). The current §47-2002(a)(7) text read contains NO holiday exception. Therefore: the holiday ran in 2024; whether it was re-enacted for 2025 or 2026 was not verified in current Code text VERIFY — secondary sources likewise say 2025/2026 holiday dates were not verified. Do not advertise a 2026/2027 holiday.

Deposit destination — history and repeal trap. The 2022 act directed proceeds to the Healthy DC Fund through 30 Sep 2026, with revenue above the FY2023-budget certified amount for FY2023–2026 to the Medical Cannabis Social Equity Fund (§7-1671.08b); from 1 Oct 2026 all proceeds to the Social Equity Fund. That scheme is overtaken: current §47-2002(a)(7)(B) (FY2026) deposits to the Healthy DC Fund with excess to the General Fund, and §7-1671.08b is now [Repealed]. The post-FY2026 deposit rule (from 1 Oct 2026) was not stated in the §47-2002 excerpt read VERIFY — do not assume it remains the Social Equity Fund.

2.2 Gifting shops and “gifts” — tax treatment

There is no lawful “gift” tax treatment for commercial gifting shops, because the underlying transaction is unlawful. A genuine non-commercial transfer without remuneration (§48-904.01(a)(1)(B)) has no sale and no gross receipts, so no sales tax arises. A shop that sells an unrelated item and “gifts” cannabis is, under Chapter 16B, an unlicensed establishment selling/exchanging cannabis as part of a commercial transaction (§7-1671.01(22)); it is subject to the $10,000/$20,000/$30,000 fines, cease-and-desist, nuisance action and summary padlocking (§7-1671.08), and — as a business — to ordinary DC business-tax obligations (business licence, income/franchise tax, sales tax on the non-cannabis item actually sold). No OTR guidance specifically taxing “gifted” cannabis was found UNAVAILABLE; do not invent a valuation rule for gifted cannabis.

2.3 Can DC tax adult-use sales under the rider? No — in practice

Subsection (b) of the rider bars use of ANY District funds “to enact any law, rule, or regulation to legalize or otherwise reduce penalties … for recreational purposes.” A tax on adult-use sales presupposes a law authorising/regulating those sales; enacting and administering such a tax requires District expenditure (OTR collection, ABCA licensing/regulation). DC therefore cannot currently enact an adult-use sales tax. (Theoretical edge: a tax enacted with zero District expenditure is not a realistic reading and no DC authority adopts it.) Past Council bills that assumed 13–17% adult-use taxes (secondary reporting) were proposals only, never enacted, and are not law.

2.4 IRS §280E and DC decoupling — under the federal split

Federal (baseline, given): §280E disallows deductions/credits for a business trafficking in Schedule I/II substances. After 91 FR 22714 (28 Apr 2026): FDA-approved cannabis products + state-licensed medical marijuana = Schedule III → §280E “generally removes as a bar” (Treasury, guidance forthcoming) for that activity. All adult-use marijuana = Schedule I → §280E fully applies. Unlicensed activity = Schedule I → fully applies.

DC decoupling (District income tax only):

  • Enacted: Medical Cannabis Amendment Act of 2022 (D.C. Law 24-332) — §47-1803.03(a)(1) proviso (quoted in Part 0): a “licensed medical cannabis business” (defined §47-1801.04(31A): ABCA-licensed cultivation centre, manufacturer, retailer, internet retailer, courier or testing laboratory) “shall be allowed to, for the purposes of District income taxes, claim any tax deduction or credit that is prohibited for purposes of filing federal income taxes under §280E.”
  • The enrolled act also added a deduction for licensed retailers/internet retailers for lost gross income from reduced-fee (sliding-scale) patient sales under §7-1671.05(b)(16)(B) — per the enrolled-act snippet; current Code location VERIFY.
  • Applicability caveat VERIFY — MATERIAL: FindLaw presents the proviso as the version “applicable upon the date of inclusion of the fiscal effect of amendments by D.C. Law 24-332 in an approved budget and financial plan,” with the pre-inclusion version lacking the proviso; the DC Law Library current text read in this run does NOT show the proviso in (a)(1). OCFO's fiscal impact statement for B24-0113 projected reduced income-tax revenue from the deduction of $1.869m (FY2024), $1.962m (FY2025), $2.060m (FY2026) — suggesting the fiscal effect was contemplated, but whether/when it was “included in an approved budget and financial plan” (triggering the proviso) was not confirmed from an OTR/OCFO source in this run. Do not tell a business the DC deduction is available until OTR confirms applicability for the relevant tax year.

Effect under the split (analysis, applying baseline + DC law):

  • DC-licensed medical activity, if within the federal Schedule III carve-out: federal §280E already inapplicable; the DC proviso is redundant for federal purposes and confirmatory for DC purposes.
  • DC-licensed medical activity, if the carve-out is read as “state”-only and DC excluded: federal §280E still applies federally; the DC proviso (if applicable) shelters only the District return — the business still faces federal §280E.
  • Adult-use activity (no lawful DC adult-use market in any event) and unlicensed activity: federal §280E fully applies; the DC proviso does NOT help (it covers only “licensed medical cannabis business” activity, and unlicensed sellers are not licensed medical businesses).
  • Mixed businesses must segregate medical vs adult-use/Schedule I income and expenses — no DC apportionment rule for mixed activity was found VERIFY.

2.5 Tax revenue figures (official data only — read the caution)

Caution: No OTR-published annual medical-cannabis sales-tax collection series was opened in this run. The figures below are therefore not presented as an OTR series. An official tax-revenue series is UNAVAILABLE in this run — the correct next step is an OTR/OCFO tax-collection publication or a data request, not secondary repetition.
  • OCFO projection (not collections): Healthy DC Fund to receive ~$2.2m in FY2023 from medical cannabis sales (OCFO FIS for B24-0113). OCFO fiscal impact statement (PDF)
  • Secondary series (CannBus, citing official sources — SECONDARY ONLY, verify against OTR/OCFO before use): FY2022 $2,229,000; FY2023 $2,218,672; FY2024 $1,779,189 (DC fiscal years ending 30 Sep). At 6%, those imply retail sales of ~$37.2m / $37.0m / $29.7m respectively — arithmetic only, not an official sales series, and inconsistent with ABCA's later monthly programme-wide totals (which include wholesale cultivation/manufacturer sales and therefore exceed retail sales). Do not mix retail-sales-tax-implied figures with programme-wide sales figures.
  • Illustrative (not a collection figure): 6% × ABCA August 2026 dispensary sales ($6,489,456) = $389,367 for that month's retail sales, before any holiday/exemption — arithmetic, not OTR data.

Part 3 — Local rules

3.1 No counties or municipalities — wards and ANCs instead

DC is a single federal district with no counties, cities or municipalities beneath it. Local input runs through:

  • Eight wards, each electing a Councilmember (plus 4 at-large members and the Chairman). Ward is a field on every ABCA/GIS facility record and on ABCA's retailer finder (“Retailers are also listed by ward below the map”).
  • Advisory Neighborhood Commissions (ANCs) — hyper-local elected bodies, subdivided into Single Member Districts (SMDs). “Affected ANC” = any ANC within 600 feet of a cultivation/manufacturing/retail/internet-retail facility (§7-1671.01(1E)).
  • ANC role in licensing (statutory, not advisory-only): ABCA must give electronic notice to the ward Councilmember + any affected ANC ≥45 calendar days before approving a retailer location (§7-1671.06h); ANC + applicant may jointly extend the protest deadline up to 30 calendar days to vote; the Board “shall accord great weight” to affected ANC input (§7-1671.06j); ANCs (and abutting/across-the-street owners/tenants, and daycare centres within 400 ft) have standing to protest retailer issuance/renewal/transfer (§7-1671.06g) on Chapter 16B violation, directly-related civil violation, or vehicular/pedestrian safety (§7-1671.06i); for unlicensed-establishment transition applications, ANC protest grounds are peace/order/quiet, residential parking + vehicular/pedestrian safety, and real property values (§7-1671.06a(i)), with a contested-case hearing within 120 days of a timely protest; a stipulated licence requires written ANC-officer correspondence that the ANC voted (quorum present) to support or not object (§7-1671.06(z)(1)(B)), and the stipulated licence stops if a valid ANC protest is filed in the 45-day protest or 30-day extended comment period. B26-0522 would extend ANC protest rights to internet retailer licences (pending — Part 1 §1.10).

ANCs do NOT issue licences, set tax, or zone — those are Council/ABCA/Zoning Commission functions. ANC “great weight” is not a veto; the Board may issue without a hearing if a protest lacks substance (§7-1671.06i(c)).

3.2 Moratoriums, caps, restrictions by ward

  • Statutory cap/moratorium power: §7-1671.06(i) — the Board shall issue rules within 180 days after 22 Mar 2023 for requesting/reviewing/implementing a cap or moratorium on cultivation/retailer/internet-retailer licences in a Ward, ANC or SMD; after one year after 22 Mar 2023 the Board may by rulemaking limit numbers in a Ward/ANC/SMD; the Board must analyse/consider supply and demand. Whether any ward/ANC/SMD cap or moratorium is currently in force under 22-C96 or Board rulemaking was NOT confirmed in this run VERIFY — 22-C96 (“Moratoriums”) exists in the DCMR index but its text was not opened. Do not assert any ward is capped.
  • De facto distribution limits (not ward caps): the 400-ft retailer-to-retailer separation (§7-1671.06(q-1)) and 400-ft (300-ft grandfathered) school/recreation-centre buffers (§7-1671.06(q)) constrain density everywhere (see §3.5).
  • Per-licence holding caps (not ward caps): max 2 cultivation licences; combined retailer+internet ≤3; cultivation holder max 1 retailer, no internet retailer; lab holders and courier holders subject to exclusivity (§7-1671.06(e)).
  • Social-equity set-aside: ≥50% of new retailer/internet/courier/cultivation/ manufacturer licences after 22 Mar 2023 (§7-1671.06(h)) — a programme-wide set-aside, not a ward quota.

3.3 Official list/map of licensed businesses by ward and type

Primary source (interactive map + ward list, no bulk download on the page itself): ABCA, “Find a Medical Cannabis Retailer” — abca.dc.gov/service/find-medical-cannabis-retailer. “Only ABCA licensed medical cannabis Retailers are included on the map. Retailers are also listed by ward below the map.” “DC features more than 70 physical and online licensed retailers” (page text, undated — conflicts with the dated August 2026 report's 66 total retailers; see the Part 5 decision). Retailers operate daily 7:00 a.m.–11:00 p.m. (hours may vary as approved); internet retailers deliver only, no in-person/curbside pickup. “Retailers that have been verified as operational will appear on this list.”

Partial rendering: the JS map/list did not fully render in the text fetch, so the ward list below is not a complete list and must not be presented as one. For a complete, current list, use the ABCA map page itself or the GIS datasets in Part 5.
  • Internet Retailers (online only): Budz & Blooms, Local'd.
  • Ward 1: All the Buzz (3232 Georgia Ave NW), All Vybez DC (3011 Georgia Ave NW), Apple Dream Shop (3106 Mount Pleasant St NW), Apple Dream Shop Columbia (1748 Columbia Road NW), Best Stop Dispensary (3439 14th St NW).
  • Ward 2: (list not rendered in fetch).
  • Ward 3: Aligned (3715 Macomb St NW).
  • Ward 4: HotBox Georgetown (6301 Georgia Ave NW — note: the address is in Ward 4 geography though the trade name says Georgetown; ward assignment as rendered on the page), Kush Embassy (7350 Georgia Ave NW), MetroLeaf (3919 14th Street NW).
  • Ward 5: Chillville Wellness (2113 Rhode Island Ave NE), Higher Ground (1135 Okie Street NE), Lucid X Dreams (1334 North Capitol St NW).

  • Ward 7: CREEP Cannabis Co. (2924 Minnesota Ave SE), The Good Joint (4723 Sheriff Rd NE).
  • Ward 8: Therapy (2923 Martin Luther King Jr. Ave SE).

The August 2026 ABCA report does not publish a by-ward retailer count; by-ward counts for unlicensed closures (113 total) are in Part 0 / Part 6.

Source: ABCA 2024 tax-holiday release (abca.dc.gov/node/1718186). Do not use as current.

3.4 Additional permits on top of the cannabis licence

From §7-1671.06(k)(2) (conditional licence conversion) and §7-1671.06a eligibility, a facility must produce, as applicable:

  • Lease or similar documentation for the proposed location;
  • Security plan (assessed by MPD for licensing standards, §7-1671.05(b)(17)(B));
  • Zoning certificate OR certificate of occupancy for the proposed location (Act 26-391 clarified a zoning certificate may substitute for a certificate of occupancy);
  • Business licence — unlicensed-establishment transition applicants needed a valid, active business licence issued on or before 31 Dec 2022 + valid certificate of occupancy + operation since 31 Dec 2022 at the latest + proof DC business taxes paid for each year since licence/occupancy (§7-1671.06a(a)(2)–(4));
  • DC incorporation as a for-profit or nonprofit corporation (§7-1671.06(s)(1));
  • Any remaining licensing/endorsement fees + any other Board-requested documentation.

Agency names: the Department of Licensing and Consumer Protection (DLCP) is the successor to DCRA for business licensing; the Department of Buildings (DOB) handles zoning certificates/occupancy-related functions. Exact current DLCP/DOB application URLs and fee schedules were not opened in this run VERIFY — do not link from memory. Fire/EMS, OTR and DLCP enforcement notifications to the Board are required within 30 days (§7-1671.12d), confirming those agencies' parallel jurisdiction.

3.5 Zones where cannabis businesses are permitted; zoning/buffer rules

Statutory buffers (current, incl. Act 26-391 emergency text):

  • Retailer/internet retailer: NOT in any residential district AND NOT within 400 feet of a preschool, primary or secondary school, or recreation centre — except a licence holder/applicant who applied before 17 Dec 2024 is held to 300 feet; and an unlicensed-establishment applicant from the 90-day period may locate within 300 feet where the school/recreation centre's main entrance is actually on / occupies ground zoned commercial or industrial per Zoning Commission atlases (§7-1671.06(q)(1)). A compliant licensee need not relocate on renewal if a school/recreation centre later locates within 400 ft (§7-1671.06(q)(2)).
  • Retailer-to-retailer: no retailer licence within 400 feet of another retailer-licensed facility; the Board ensures a new application is not within 400 ft of a previously submitted timely retailer application; first-in-time proceeds, then second, etc. (§7-1671.06(q-1)).
  • Daycare: the Board must also consider, for initial issuance or transfer to a new location: proximity to a daycare centre, effect on its operation/clientele, and whether school-aged children frequenting it will be unduly attracted (§7-1671.06(q-2)); summer garden endorsement barred within 400 ft of a daycare centre (§7-1671.06f(b-1)); daycare centres within 400 ft have protest standing (§7-1671.06g(a)(3)).
  • Cultivation/manufacturing location limits in transition: not in a residential district, not within 300 ft of a preschool/primary/secondary school/recreation centre (for 90-day applicants, §7-1671.06a(a)(2)); the facility must be suitable (size, power, air exchange/airflow) for cultivation.
  • Distance measurement: DCMR 22-C Ch. 50 historically provided linear measurement as the shortest distance between property lines (2018 compilation) — current 22-C50 text VERIFY; do not rely on the 2018 compilation as current.

Zoning use categories (Zoning Administrator Interpretation ZA-031, Department of Buildings): Residential districts where medical cannabis businesses (except couriers) are prohibited: R (Residential House), RF (Residential Flat), RA (Residential Apartment), CG-1 (Capitol Gateway), D-1 (Downtown), BF (Barry Farm) zones. Permitted use categories:

BusinessUse categoryWhere permitted
Manufacturer Type 1PDRAny non-residential zone permitting PDR/light manufacturing
Manufacturer Type 2PDRPDR zones only
Cultivation CenterPDRAny non-residential zone permitting PDR/light manufacturing
RetailerRetailAny non-residential zone permitting Retail use

Full zone chart (Exhibit 1, PDF): dob.dc.gov — Exhibit 1; interpretation: ZA-031. Currency caveat: ZA-031 predates Act 26-391's 400-ft change (it cites the older 300-ft rule). The statute prevails over the interpretation where they differ — an updated ZA-031 was not found VERIFY.

3.6 Business concentration — only what official sources say

  • ABCA's August 2026 report (official) gives unlicensed-closure concentration, not licensed-business concentration: Ward 1 (36) and Ward 6 (17) account for 53 of 113 cumulative closures; Ward 3 (5) and Ward 8 (6) the fewest. That is an enforcement footprint, not a licensed-market cluster ranking, and must not be re-labelled as one.
  • ABCA's retailer finder organises retailers by ward but publishes no ward counts and no concentration analysis in the text fetched.
  • No official regulator report, map or dashboard stating licensed-business concentration/clustering was found in this run. Any cluster ranking would be analyst estimation — not done here. The GIS datasets in Part 5 allow a reader to compute ward counts themselves from official point data, but this dossier does not present such a computation as an official finding.

Part 4 — Licence types

General rules (all types). A licence is required before cultivating, manufacturing, distributing, dispensing, delivering, selling, possessing or testing (§7-1671.06(b)). Licences are valid 3 years (unless suspended/revoked or mid-cycle issuance) (§7-1671.06(n)(3)); annual fees payable (first year within 60 calendar days of Board approval but before issuance; renewals on or before the anniversary) (§7-1671.06(n)(1)); late fee $50/business day, capped at licence cost; returned-payment fee $100 (§7-1671.06(n)(4)–(5)). Applicants must be DC-incorporated for-profit/nonprofit corporations (§7-1671.06(s)(1)); security plan + locked room; employee compliance training. Applications are taken only during open application periods, EXCEPT testing laboratory licences, certification training provider permits, and retailer endorsements, which are accepted on an ongoing basis (ABCA Licenses page). Applications outside a stated period “will be rejected without notice”; application fees are payable within 1 business day of formal acceptance notice (ABCA page).

ABCA hub: Medical Cannabis Business Licenses; application page: Business License and Endorsement Application.

Licence / endorsementWhat it allows Canopy / plant limitsAnnual fee — standard / social equity Application feeCaps & status
Cultivation Center (Tiers 1–6) Cultivate + possess for distribution to manufacturer/retailer/internet retailer; manufacture/possess/purchase/use products + paraphernalia (§7-1671.06(a)(1)). ABCA: “growing of cannabis for sale to licensed Retailers in DC for resale to eligible patients” (note: the statute also allows sale to manufacturers/internet retailers). Courier Third-party delivery on behalf of licensed retailers/internet retailers to patients/caregivers (§7-1671.01(4A); ABCA). Uses own employees/independent contractors; platform/app-based. None $8,000 / $2,000 $4,000 standard VERIFY — secondary only May hold NO retailer/internet/cultivation/lab/manufacturer licence (§7-1671.06(e)(1)(B)). Open periods only.
Testing Laboratory Possess for testing contents (§7-1671.06(a)(5)); report THC/CBD, organic/inorganic, fertilisers/nutrients, mould/mildew/pests, mycotoxin/pesticides/heavy metals (+ residual solvents for manufacturers). May also test patient samples (registered patient + licensed-retailer purchase proof) and QA/R&D samples (non-random selection allowed; QA/R&D tests do NOT satisfy mandatory batch testing) (§7-1671.05(b)(11A)). None $7,500 / $1,875 No initial application fee (§7-1671.06(n)(2)(B)); renewal fee by rule May hold NO cultivation/manufacturer/retailer/internet/courier licence; must not be owned/operated by any director/officer/member/incorporator/agent/employee of another licensee category (§7-1671.01(21)). Accepted on an ongoing basis (ABCA page). First lab licensed 16 Aug 2024 (ClearSight Labs) — triggering mandatory batch testing.
Conditional Licence (not a facility type) For applicants WITHOUT a proposed location, all types (ABCA page says “available for all license types” — statute §7-1671.06(k) covers cultivation, retailer, internet retailer, manufacturer, courier, testing laboratory). Holder may NOT purchase/possess/cultivate/manufacture/sell medical cannabis/products/Schedule I substances while conditional. Must submit lease, security plan, zoning certificate or certificate of occupancy, remaining fees + Board-requested documents by end of term, and be operating by end of term, or the licence is cancelled. NOT transferable to a new owner (§7-1671.06(l)). Per underlying type Underlying type's fees when converted. Conditional fees (secondary only): standard $800 on application + $1,200 on approval (except labs); social-equity conditional total $500 VERIFY — secondary only — Unlicensed establishments could NOT use a conditional licence to meet 90-day-period requirements (§7-1671.06a(f)). Term up to 3 years (Act 26-391 / Law 26-71; earlier 1-year → 2-year → 3-year conversions).
Certification Training Provider Permit ABCA lists it as the 1 medical cannabis business permit alongside the 6 licences. (ABCA's page says “seven (7) medical cannabis business licenses including Courier, Cultivation Center, Internet Retailer, Manufacturer, Retailer, and Testing Laboratory” — i.e. 6 named + conditional counted separately; the “seven” count is internally inconsistent with its own list, so this dossier reports the 6 facility licences + conditional + training permit and flags the ABCA count VERIFY.) — Training permit fee $300 (rulemaking §1303.3(e): Medical Cannabis Certification Training Permit $300, both standard + social equity) — Accepted on an ongoing basis (ABCA page).
Endorsements (retailer only unless stated) Delivery: $300 initial, $300/year, 3 years (§7-1671.06e(f)). Safe-use treatment facility: $1,000 initial, $2,000/year, 3 years (§7-1671.06c(f)) — ABCA rulemaking §1303.3 lists safe-use $2,000 annual (consistent). Summer garden: $300/$300, 3 years (§7-1671.06f(c)). Education tasting: $130/$130, 3 years (§7-1671.06d(g)) — rulemaking §1303.3(c) lists $130 (consistent). — See “What it allows” Endorsement application fees = the initial fees at left (statute calls them “minimum initial application fee”) Internet retailer: NO safe-use, NO education tasting, NO curbside pickup; delivery endorsement not required for internet retailers. Accepted on an ongoing basis (ABCA page).

Fee sources: Annual fees + endorsement fees + tier definitions + late/ duplicate/returned fees are from ABCA rulemaking (Technical Third Proposed-Emergency, Dec 2023, §1303–1304, and the 6th emergency rulemaking Nov 2024 restating §1303 identically): Dec 2023 rulemaking (PDF) and Nov 2024 rulemaking (PDF) — these are emergency/proposed rulemakings; the current ABCA Fee and Renewal Schedule page was not directly opened in this run, so confirm no fee change since Nov 2024 VERIFY. Duplicate/replacement licence $25; returned/declined check $100 (rulemaking §1303.4–1303.5, consistent with statute). Social-equity annual fees are exactly 25% of standard (75% waiver) for the first 3 years (§7-1671.06(m)(1)); the waiver does NOT apply to endorsement fees (§7-1671.06(m)(2)). Pre-2023 licensees: cultivation centres licensed as of 10 Mar 2023 auto-received a manufacturer licence on fee payment and were eligible for a retailer licence on application + fee (applications by 1 May 2025 — historical).

Social equity + returning-citizen preferences (beyond fees)

  • Set-aside: ≥50% of new retailer/internet/courier/cultivation/manufacturer licences after 22 Mar 2023 (§7-1671.06(h)).
  • Affidavit: number of qualifying owners, ownership interests/incomes/net worth, managerial-employee office location, residency of owners/employees/contractors, asset locations/percentages (§7-1671.06(g)(1)).
  • Medical cannabis certified business enterprise (CBE): DSLBD-certified equity-impact enterprise + >50% DC-resident employees + per-owner annual personal net income ≤$349,999 attestation (§7-1671.01(12A)).
  • Straw ownership prohibited; wilful straw ownership → licence/registration revoked + civil penalty ≤$30,000 (§7-1671.06(j); §7-1671.06a(e)(2) for transition licences: revocation + ≤$30,000).
  • Criminal-record protections in licensing: see §7-1671.06(u) (Part 1 §1.2). No employee background check with application.
  • Transfer bar: social-equity/unlicensed-establishment licences not transferable to a new owner until 3 years after permanent-licence issuance (except to a social-equity applicant); later non-CBE/non-social-equity transfer requires repayment of District grants/loans + waived fees (§7-1671.06(r)(2)).
  • Grants/loans: §§7-1671.08b/08c (Social Equity Fund; equity grants/loans) are [Repealed] in current text — any current DSLBD/ABCA grant programme for social-equity applicants was not confirmed in this run VERIFY; do not promise grant funding.

How unlicensed gifting shops could transition (historical window — now closed as a programme)

  1. 90-day open application period under §7-1671.06a (no earlier than 180 days after 22 Mar 2023; ABCA accepted applications in 2023–24 — the May 2023 unlicensed-operator instructions and the 1 July 2024 eligibility date in §7-1671.06(b-1) mark this era). Eligibility: not in a residential district; not within 300 ft of a preschool/primary/secondary school/recreation centre (retailer also not within 400 ft of an existing retailer; internet retailer not open to the public + same buffers); valid active business licence on/before 31 Dec 2022; valid certificate of occupancy before application; in operation since 31 Dec 2022 at the latest; DC business taxes paid each year since licence/occupancy; suitable cultivation facility (cultivation applicants).
  2. While the application was pending: protected from Chapter 16B compliance EXCEPT child-appealing products/packaging/ads (§7-1671.06b(a)) — that protection ended in practice 1 Apr 2025 for imminent-danger summary closure (§7-1671.06b(a-1)), and Act 26-391 (2026) confirms enforcement against all unlicensed establishments regardless of a pending application for Schedule I sales (§7-1671.08(c-1)).
  3. On approval: written decision ≥15 days before issuance; cease all unlicensed activity on issuance; open within 120 days (retailer/internet) or the licence is cancelled; retailer/internet in operation by 31 Mar 2025 or approval rescinded (historical deadlines).
  4. ABCA was required to post the 90-day applicant list on its website + give a copy to DLCP (§7-1671.06a(g)) — that list's current URL was not retrieved in this run VERIFY (Part 5).
  5. Outcome (Mayor, Jan 2026): “more than 90 businesses chose that path forward” (licensing) before enforcement; the rest faced closure. No new unlicensed-establishment transition window is open on sources found VERIFY on ABCA's open-application schedule. A shop that missed the window must now apply as a standard/social-equity applicant in a future open period (if any) and must NOT operate unlicensed in the meantime.

Estimated startup costs — NON-OFFICIAL, not used as official figures: No ABCA/official startup-cost estimate was found. The licence fees above are the only official cost figures. Build-out, security, inventory, testing (per-batch lab fees — lab fee schedule UNAVAILABLE), insurance, rent, and Metrc costs are business-specific and unquantified officially. Any “$X to open a DC dispensary” figure from consultants is non-official and excluded rather than repeated.

Part 5 — Licence data sources

An inventory of every source of licence-holder and programme data in DC — not a list of licence holders. Conflicting-figures rule applied: where counts conflict, the dated ABCA monthly programme report is treated as most authoritative for its month (it is the regulator's own operational data), the undated ABCA finder page second, and secondary aggregations last. Conflicts are stated, not averaged.

Total licence counts by type/status — decision under the conflicting-figures rule

  • Retailers: 66 total (ABCA August 2026 report, dated, most authoritative). The ABCA finder page's “more than 70 physical and online licensed retailers” is undated and counts “verified as operational” listings; the September 2026 secondary report says 67 retailers. Use 66 for August 2026; note the finder-page >70 and September 67 as conflicting/undated or secondary, not as corrections.
  • Cultivation centres, manufacturers, couriers, testing labs by count: VERIFY from a dated ABCA source. Secondary figures conflict sharply and are NOT adopted: CannBus Q3 2026 (8 cultivation, 10 manufacturers, 1 lab, 1 courier, 66 retailers reporting Aug 2026) vs a December 2025 secondary market page (85 operators: 3 cultivation, 3 manufacturers, 6 combined cultivation-manufacturing, 73 dispensaries, 2 online-only, 1 courier, 1 lab — and 94,067 “unique registered patients,” which contradicts ABCA's 143,556 registered residents in Aug 2026 and likely counts a different patient definition or an earlier/stale snapshot). The August 2026 ABCA report excerpt opened does not tabulate cultivation/manufacturer/lab/courier counts. Do not publish a by-type total table from secondary sources.
  • Statuses: GIS non-retailer data distinguishes active (operating) vs safekeeping (temporarily closed) — the August 2026 report's retailer swing (69 in June → 65 in July → 66 in August) is consistent with safekeeping/closure churn, but the report does not label the change as such — reason VERIFY.
  • Conditional licences: no current count found UNAVAILABLE.

Interactive-only sources (no download)

ABCA retailer finder/map (5.2); ABCA Board search (5.5, per-document PDFs only); MyABCA portal (registration/licensing transactions, login-gated — not a public data source). GIS non-retailer dataset downloads are via the ArcGIS Hub item (5.3) — CSV/GeoJSON/Shapefile/KML endpoints are generated by the Hub; the catalog record confirms those formats exist but the final per-format file URLs were not captured in this run VERIFY on the Hub page before downloading.

Part 6 — Market data

Critical reading note — programme-wide vs retail sales: ABCA's “Total” monthly sales = dispensaries + cultivation centres + manufacturers. The same cannabis is therefore counted at wholesale (cultivator/manufacturer → retailer) and again at retail. Programme-wide totals are NOT retail market size and must not be compared directly with retail-only series from other jurisdictions or with tax-implied retail sales. Dispensary sales are the retail proxy.

6.2 Registered patient counts (including self-certified)

ABCA August 2026 report (official, dated):

  • Total registered DC resident patients: June 131,133 → July 136,882 → August 143,556.
  • Total unique caregivers: 32 (July 32, June 28).
  • Total unique patients served by a registered dispensary: June 41,056 → July 40,863 → August 39,382. Note: this “served” total does not equal the sum of the three purchaser groups below (DC-resident purchasers 25,005 + reciprocity 2,233 + temporary self-certified purchasers 13,553 = 40,791 in August, vs 39,382 “served”) — the report does not define the difference (de-duplication across groups is the likely explanation, but that is inference — definition VERIFY).
  • DC-resident purchasers (August): 25,005 unique patients, 69,635 unique sales (67,454 in-store, 2,181 delivery), average sale $58 ($56 in-store, $125 delivery), in-store $3,790,594 + delivery $272,152 = $4,062,746.
  • Reciprocity purchasers (August): 2,233 unique patients, 4,517 sales, average $100, total $453,559. Top residencies: MD 899, VA 815, FL 165, PA 48, NY 22.
  • Temporary self-certified non-resident purchasers (August): 12,144 registered in-month (July 12,599, June 12,849 — declining); 13,553 unique purchasers; 21,009 sales; average $94; total $1,973,150. Top temporary-registration residencies: VA 4,334, MD 1,740, NC 834, TX 515, FL 488.
  • Patient expirations (upcoming): September/October/November all shown as 0 in the August report — an artefact of the 2-year (and legacy 6-year) validity structure, not a claim that no registrations expire; do not interpret as zero expirations.
  • Self-certified vs practitioner-recommended split: NOT published in the August report excerpts VERIFY — the report splits by residency/reciprocity/temporary status, not by certification route. The scale of self-certification can be inferred from the temporary non-resident self-certified volumes and the resident growth since 2022, but a precise self-certified headcount is not stated officially in sources opened.
  • Historical context (ABCA releases, secondary-confirmed): a 2025 ABCA/MPD release cited a 142.5% increase in registered DC + non-resident patients vs March 2024, with 56 retailers + 2 internet retailers at that time (58 total) — that release's date/figures are a secondary reprint VERIFY; the direction (explosive growth after self-certification + retailer expansion from ~17 operational in early 2025 to 66 in Aug 2026) is consistent across ABCA reports.

Part 7 — Hemp licences

DC has no hemp licence of any kind — no grower, processor, handler or retailer licence, no DC hemp programme, and no USDA-approved DC plan. The reason is structural: DC law never carved hemp out of “cannabis.”

7.1 Bottom line

QuestionAnswer
DC hemp grower licence? None issued; no programme to issue one. Secondary sources state cultivation/processing are prohibited in DC and DC “cannot issue a hemp growing or processing license.”
DC hemp processor / handler / retailer licence? No hemp-specific licence type exists. A business that wants to handle cannabinoids in DC must fit the medical cannabis licence system (Cultivation, Manufacturer, Retailer, Internet Retailer, Courier, Testing Laboratory — Part 4), not a hemp licence.
DC's own programme, USDA programme, or no production programme? No production programme. DC is absent from USDA's approved-plan list (updated 17 February 2026, opened in Run 2). It is listed neither as “Approved Plan” nor as “USDA Hemp Producer License.”
Hemp registry? None found. No DC hemp registry, grower list or handler list was located on ABCA, USDA or DC government pages searched in Run 2. This reconciles with Part 5 only if Part 5 likewise found no registry; if Part 5 listed a registry, that would be a conflict — see Gap C-02 in the research notes.
Fees for hemp licences Not applicable — no licence to fee. Any fee quoted for a “DC hemp licence” by a commercial site should be treated as unreliable.
Info link (official, for hemp status) USDA approved-plan list: ams.usda.gov/rules-regulations/hemp/state-and-tribal-plan-review — DC does not appear in the States, Territories or Tribes tables. USDA grower information: ams.usda.gov/rules-regulations/hemp/information-for-hemp-growers

7.2 Why DC has no hemp programme — the legal chain

1. D.C. Code §48-901.02(3) — no hemp exemption (Part 1 baseline, confirmed in Run 2)

“‘Cannabis’ means all parts of the plant genus Cannabis, including both marijuana and hashish…”

“Marijuana” is defined to include “the leaves, stems, flowers, and seeds of all species of the plant genus Cannabis, whether growing or not” (excluding mature stalks/fibre, seed oil/cake and sterilised seed incapable of germination).

D.C. Code §48-901.02, as reproduced at codes.findlaw.com and law.justia.com (index, accessed 24 October 2026). There is no 0.3% delta-9 THC hemp carve-out in that definition. The D.C. Law Library official Code should be re-checked for the current consolidated text — Gap C-03.

2. D.C. Code §7-1671.01(2A) adopts the same definition for the medical programme

“‘Cannabis’ shall have the same meaning as provided in §48-901.02(3).”

D.C. Code Title 7, Chapter 16B (opened, 24 October 2026)

3. OAG memorandum AL-21-222 (28 April 2021)

Part 1 baseline: treats hemp-derived CBD as cannabis. Run 2 did not re-open the memorandum itself; it is carried forward as baseline, not re-verified. Secondary legal analysis in Run 2 repeats the same conclusion (“CBD is cannabis”). Gap C-04: obtain the memorandum PDF from OAG and quote it.

4. Court confirmation — DeLorean 88 LLC v. District of Columbia

No. 1:2025cv02458, Memorandum Opinion 8 October 2025 (Judge McFadden, D.D.C.). The court states:

“The District qualifies as a ‘state’ for purposes of the 2018 Farm Bill but does not have a USDA-approved hemp plan.”

“In its laws, the District does not distinguish between ‘hemp’ and ‘marijuana.’ Instead, D.C. law generally prohibits selling, possessing, or manufacturing ‘cannabis.’ D.C. Code §48-904.01(a)(1) & (d)(1).”

law.justia.com — DeLorean 88 LLC v. District of Columbia (index, 24 October 2026)

The court rejected the argument that the 2018 Farm Bill creates a federal right to possess/store/sell federally defined hemp in DC, and upheld DC law as applied to intra-District hemp possession/sale. It also noted a state/tribe without a USDA-approved plan is governed by USDA regulations under 7 U.S.C. §1639q(a)(1) — but that federal production framework does not override DC's own prohibition on sale/possession outside the medical programme, as applied in this case. Related earlier litigation noted in secondary analysis: Capitol Hemp, LLC v. United States — details not verified in Run 2; Gap C-05.

5. USDA position (opened, 24 October 2026)

USDA's list, updated 17 February 2026, covers every state, Guam/Puerto Rico/U.S. Virgin Islands, and a long tribal list — DC appears in none of them. USDA's grower page states the general rule: to produce hemp you must be licensed/authorised under a state, tribal or USDA programme, depending on facility location; if the state/tribe has no pending/approved plan you may apply for a USDA licence via HeMP. That general rule does not create a DC-law right: a USDA licence, if one were ever issued for a DC site, would still collide with D.C. Code §§48-904.01 and 7-1671.01 et seq., and no DC site licence was found in Run 2. Whether USDA would even accept a DC-site application, given DC's absence from its table, is VERIFY — check USDA HeMP / farmbill.hemp@usda.gov (the contact given on USDA's grower page).

Sources: USDA approved-plan list; USDA information for hemp growers (both opened/index, 24 October 2026).

7.3 What each (non-existent) hemp category means in practice

CategoryDC positionPractical consequence
Grower / cultivator No licence, no plan Growing Cannabis spp. in DC outside (a) home cultivation under Initiative 71 (≤6 plants, ≤3 mature, household cap 12/6, inside principal residence only) or (b) a licensed medical cultivation centre is unlawful. A “hemp” label does not change that.
Processor / manufacturer No hemp licence Processing cannabinoids requires a medical Manufacturer licence (Part 4: $4,000, or $8,000 with extraction).
Handler / transporter No hemp handler licence Transport of medical cannabis is under 22-C DCMR §5621 (transporter licence per vehicle — see Part 11.4). Hemp in transit through DC is a different question: the 2018 Farm Bill bars a state from prohibiting transport/shipment of lawfully produced hemp through the state; DC's own transit rule for hemp was not found — Gap C-06.
Retailer No hemp retailer licence Retail sale of cannabinoid products requires a medical Retailer / Internet Retailer licence and a registered patient/caregiver buyer. Unlicensed “hemp” / “CBD” storefront sales are enforced as unlicensed cannabis sales (see Part 12.1).
Testing laboratory (hemp) No hemp-specific lab Only the medical testing laboratory licence exists (ClearSight Labs, see Part 11.1).

7.4 Delta-8 and other intoxicating hemp cannabinoids

DC treatment SECONDARY ONLY: Secondary sources state DC's controlled-substances list names delta-8 THC specifically and DC has no broad hemp exemption shielding intoxicating hemp cannabinoids, so delta-8 and similar converted cannabinoids are treated as controlled substances locally. Run 2 did not open the current DC controlled-substances schedule (§48-902.04 et seq.) to verify the delta-8 listing verbatim — Gap C-07. Do not quote a schedule entry without that check.

The practical line in DC: Because there is no hemp exemption, the question is not “is this hemp-derived?” but “is this authorised under Chapter 16B or Initiative 71?” If neither, sale is an unlicensed cannabis sale. That is why DC's practical exposure to the federal intoxicating-hemp market was already smaller than in open-hemp states (secondary analysis, 24 October 2026: JD Supra — Navigating Cannabis Regulation in Washington, D.C.).

No DC response found to the new federal hemp definition UNAVAILABLE. No DC Council bill, ABCA notice, OAG opinion or Mayor's statement responding to P.L. 119-37 §781 or P.L. 119-103 §2019 was located in Run 2 searches of ABCA, LIMS and DC government pages. This confirms Part 1's finding for DC response. Under the federal baseline as given: the total-THC (including THCA) definition and the 0.4 mg total-THC-per-container cap for final hemp-derived cannabinoid products take legal effect 12 November 2026, but P.L. 119-103 §2019 limits it until 11 December 2026 to only provisions excluding cannabinoids not naturally produced or synthesised; the full definition applies only after 11 December 2026. D.C. Code §48-901.02 is not amended by the federal change — DC would still need its own amendment to create a hemp exemption. Whether Congress's DC appropriations/control powers or the federal definition changes DC enforcement practice after 11 December 2026 is VERIFY — no DC source says so.

USDA DEA-lab testing delay (federal baseline, not re-researched): enforcement of DEA-registered-laboratory hemp testing delayed until 31 December 2026. No DC hemp testing programme exists for it to apply to.

7.5 Hemp registry reconciliation

Run 2 searched ABCA's medical cannabis pages, USDA's plan list and grower-contact list, and general web search for a “DC hemp registry / hemp licence list.” None was found. If Part 5 listed a registry, Run 2 could not confirm it and it should be treated as a conflict pending re-attachment of Part 5. The only DC cannabis registries that do exist are the medical programme's: ABCA's licensed-business lists and patient registration system (see Parts 5, 9 and 11).

8.2 What was searched — and what was NOT found

  • LoopNet / commercial search: Searches for “DC cannabis property for sale zoned dispensary LoopNet” returned only out-of-District properties (e.g. 12100 Annapolis Rd, Glenn Dale, MD — CGO zoning permitting a cannabis dispensary in Maryland; properties in MI, CA, IL). No DC LoopNet cannabis listing was returned. LoopNet's DC retail listings that appeared as “nearby” (e.g. 716–718 Kennedy St NW, 3222 11th St NW, 1821 M St NW) carried no cannabis claim in the results.
  • 420 Property: The only DC hit across its retail, cultivation and general business-for-sale indexes was 1840 Fenwick St NE (above). No DC dispensary real-estate listing with a stated price was returned.
  • DC zoning maps / DOB: DC's zoning rules for medical cannabis are in the Part 1/Part 3 baseline (400 ft school/recreation-centre buffer, 300 ft grandfathered for pre-17 December 2024 applicants; 400 ft retailer-to-retailer) and in ABCA application instructions (testing-lab applicants must show the site is not in a residential district and not within 300 ft of a preschool/primary/secondary school or recreation centre, with a certified surveyor's report or ABCA GIS map, plus a DOB Zoning Administrator determination letter and certificate of occupancy). No publicly searchable “cannabis-zoned parcels” map or ABCA property list was located — the GIS map is issued on application, not published as a property search. Gap C-08: check DOB's zoning map / Zoning Information System and ABCA's “find a retailer” / licensed-premises lists for a parcel-level check on any specific address.
  • Broker / “green zone” claims: DC does not use a California-style “green zone” designation. Any listing calling DC property “green zone” should be treated with suspicion unless it cites a DOB determination.
  • Important caveat for any buyer: A cannabis business sale is not a licence sale. Under §7-1671.06(e) and 22-C DCMR, transfers to a new owner and changes of location require Board approval (see Part 11.2), and a social-equity licence has transfer restrictions (see Part 11.2). A property being “zoned” or “previously licensed” does not guarantee a new licence.

  • 36425 Bankside Dr, Cathedral City, CA; 1003 N Elm St, Tekonsha, MI; 44518 State Highway 74, Hemet, CA — all out-of-District.
  • Washington State listings (e.g. Oroville/Longview, WA) — Washington State ≠ Washington, DC.

Department of Energy and Environment (DOEE) Pesticide registration for cannabis cultivation (22-C DCMR §5620.5: any pesticide used must be registered with DOEE and used per DOEE regulations and label); environmental/air-quality aspects; testing-lab applicants must file an environmental plan (carbon footprint, alternative energy, wastewater/runoff, air scrubbing/treatment, co-location). doee.dc.gov (domain only; Gap C-09).
Metropolitan Police Department (MPD) Joint ABCA/MPD closure enforcement (padlocks, search warrants, seizures, arrests); 24-hour loss/theft/destruction notification goes to ABCA and the MPD Chief (§7-1671.05); transport/disposal coordination (see Part 11.5); impaired-driving enforcement; public-consumption enforcement. mpdc.dc.gov (domain only). MPD's “Facts on DC Marijuana Laws” page is cited by secondary sources but was not opened in Run 2 — Gap C-10.
Office of the Attorney General (OAG) Prosecutes public-consumption violations and DUIs (OAG impaired-driving page); issued AL-21-222 on hemp-derived CBD (Part 1 baseline); represents the Board in enforcement (Assistant AG appears in Board orders); consumer/landlord and civil enforcement context. Address published on OAG site: 400 6th St NW, Washington, DC 20001, phone (202) 727-3400. oag.dc.gov — Marijuana and Impaired Driving (opened, 24 October 2026)
Department of Health (DOH) / DC Health — historical Original medical programme administrator before transfer to ABRA/ABCA (2017–2018 DOH-compiled 22-C DCMR PDFs are still the source for older security text). Current programme authority is ABCA; DOH's present cannabis role, if any (e.g. food-safety for edibles, health inspections), was not verified in Run 2. doh.dc.gov — Gap C-11.
Fire and Emergency Medical Services (FEMS) / fire code No cannabis-specific FEMS rule was found in Run 2. Fire/chemical-spill emergency procedures are required in licensee operating plans (secondary inspection checklists), and extraction/manufacturing premises are subject to general fire/building codes. Do not attribute a cannabis-specific FEMS licence to DC without a source. Gap C-12: check FEMS / DOB fire-code pages for extraction and cultivation fire requirements.
Mayor of DC Appoints ABC Board members; signs/vetoes Council cannabis legislation; transmitted testing/remediation rules to Council (24 September 2025); proposed the Medical Cannabis Beverage Product Amendment Act of 2026 (2 April 2026, per secondary source — see Part 9.2, proposed, not law). mayor.dc.gov (domain only).
Council of the District of Columbia Enacts medical cannabis law (Chapter 16B amendments), budgets, and must approve certain ABCA rules by resolution within 30 days or they are deemed approved (§7-1671.13(b)). Legislative tracking: LIMS. lims.dccouncil.gov; Code: code.dccouncil.gov
Advisory Neighborhood Commissions (ANCs) “Affected ANC” = any ANC within 600 ft of a cultivator/manufacturer/retailer/ internet-retailer facility (§7-1671.01(1E)). The Board must accord “great weight” to an affected ANC's input on a retailer location (§7-1671.06, as amended by D.C. Law 25-255). ANCs file protests and settlement agreements (e.g. ANC 3A / Top Level LLC “Aligned,” 3715 Macomb St NW, safe-use endorsement, June 2025). Via ABCA protest notices; ANC sites (anc3a.dc.gov example opened via search result).
Department of Small and Local Business Development (DSLBD) Certifies Medical Cannabis Certified Business Enterprises (CBE) / equity-impact enterprises (§7-1671.01(12A)); social-equity/CBE documentation in licence applications. dslbd.dc.gov (domain only; Gap C-09).
Department of Human Resources (DCHR) DC government drug-and-alcohol testing policy (see Part 11.8); safety-sensitive designations. edpm.dc.gov — Marijuana and the District's Drug & Alcohol Testing (index, 24 October 2026)
DC Board of Elections (BOEE) Certifies initiative results (Initiatives 59 and 71). dcboe.org (domain only; Gap C-13 for certified-result PDFs).

Federal bodies with direct authority over DC cannabis law

BodyDirect DC authority / relevance
Congress (esp. House/Senate Appropriations) Home Rule Act review of DC laws; annual appropriations riders — the Harris rider (FY2026 P.L. 119-75, enrolled section number VERIFY per Part 1) blocks DC funds to enact an adult-use sales/licensing/tax system; the earlier Barr Amendment blocked medical implementation 1998–2009. No federal cannabis banking law enacted (federal baseline). The federal medical-marijuana appropriations rider is in force through 11 December 2026 (federal baseline) — its precise application to DC (as a non-state “state” for this purpose) was not verified from rider text in Run 2; Gap C-14.
Drug Enforcement Administration (DEA) / Department of Justice CSA scheduling. Federal baseline as given: final rule 91 FR 22714, effective 28 April 2026 — FDA-approved cannabis products and state-licensed medical marijuana in Schedule III; all other marijuana including all adult-use remains Schedule I. The broader 2024 proposal is not final. Treasury/IRS position (baseline): rescheduling generally removes 280E as a bar for businesses no longer dealing in Schedule I/II substances, guidance forthcoming; adult-use remains fully subject to 280E. DC sources that contradict this must be flagged VERIFY — none was found in Run 2. ATF draft Form 4473 discussion (May 2026) acknowledging the medical Schedule III change is draft, not final — see Part 12.7.
Food and Drug Administration (FDA) FDA-approved cannabis products are the other Schedule III category in the 2026 final rule (baseline). FDA also regulates CBD in food/drugs nationally; no DC-specific FDA action was found in Run 2.
U.S. Department of Agriculture (USDA) — Agricultural Marketing Service Hemp production plans / USDA producer licences (see Part 7); DEA-lab testing enforcement delayed to 31 December 2026 (baseline). DC has no approved plan and is absent from USDA's list.
Bureau of Alcohol, Tobacco, Firearms and Explosives (ATF) Firearms prohibition for “unlawful users” (18 U.S.C. §922(g)(3); Form 4473 Q.21(f)); 2011 open letter on medical cardholders. 2026 draft Form 4473 / DOJ rights-restoration rule — not final as of Run 2 sources (see Part 12.7).
National Park Service (NPS) / U.S. Park Police; U.S. Capitol Police; military installations Enforce federal law on federal land in DC (~29% / ~18 sq mi per secondary sources, including National Mall, West/East Potomac Parks, Rock Creek Park, Anacostia Park, Capitol grounds, Smithsonian properties, Joint Base Anacostia–Bolling). DC law does not apply there. See Part 12.5.
Department of Housing and Urban Development (HUD) Federally assisted housing: PHAs/owners may not accommodate medical cannabis; admission/termination rules apply even for DC-registered patients. See Part 12.6. The Marijuana in Federally Assisted Housing Parity Act (Norton/Booker) has been repeatedly reintroduced and not enacted (secondary, 24 October 2026).
Internal Revenue Service (IRS) / Treasury 280E (baseline above); DC decoupling under D.C. Law 24-332 with an applicability proviso VERIFY per Part 2 — Run 2 did not re-open Law 24-332; Gap C-15.
U.S. Attorney for DC / DC Courts Federal prosecutions on federal land; DC Superior Court / DC Court of Appeals for DC-law enforcement, record sealing/expungement (see Part 12.3), and appeals from Board summary action.

9.3 Ballot initiative history and results

InitiativeQuestion / short titleResultNotes
Initiative 59 (Nov 1998) “Legalization of Marijuana for Medical Treatment Initiative of 1998” Passed: 75,536 Yes (68.57%) vs 34,621 No (31.43%), total 110,157 (secondary tabulation). Text in some summaries rounds to “69%.” Implementation blocked by Barr Amendment until 2009; first sale 2013. Secondary sources only for the vote table — BOEE certified result not opened (Gap C-13).
Initiative 71 (4 Nov 2014) “Legalization of Possession of Minimal Amounts of Marijuana for Personal Use Act of 2014” — 21+: possess ≤2 oz; grow ≤6 plants (≤3 mature) in principal residence; transfer ≤1 oz without payment; use/sell paraphernalia for lawful marijuana use/growing/processing. Certified: 64.87% For (Ballotpedia certified-results formulation, which counts undervotes/overvotes in the denominator). The for/against-only split in the same sources is 115,050 For (70.06%) vs 49,168 Against (29.94%), total 164,218. Conflict flagged: “64.87%” and “70.06%” are both quoted in secondary sources for the same election — they differ by denominator, not by votes. Report both with denominators, or cite the BOEE certified result. Election-night reporting quoted ~64.62% For / 28.43% Against on partial returns — do not use partial returns as final. Effective 26 Feb 2015. Sales not authorised by the initiative; the initiative itself states it does not make sale lawful. Congress's rider then blocked a sales system.
Other initiatives Initiative 62 (2002, drug treatment in lieu of prosecution for some nonviolent offenders — passed 78.17%) is drug-policy-adjacent but not a cannabis-legalisation initiative. — No other DC cannabis ballot initiative (medical or adult-use sales) was found in Run 2. A Council “tax and regulate” bill (not an initiative) has been repeatedly blocked by the rider; DC initiatives cannot appropriate funds, which is why a tax-and-regulate initiative was not viable (secondary, DCMJ history). Gap C-20: confirm the full DC ballot-measure list on BOEE / DC Council pages.
