Part 10 — Tribal and interstate

10.1 Tribes in DC — explicit finding: none with land or cannabis operations in DC

  • No federally recognised tribe has a reservation, trust land, or tribal government seat in the District of Columbia, and no tribal cannabis or hemp operation in DC was found. DC is not on any reservation list, and USDA's tribal hemp-plan list (opened, 24 October 2026) contains no DC-based tribe.
  • Ancestral / historical context (not a current legal landholding): DC sits on the ancestral land of the Nacotchtank (Anacostan) and Piscataway peoples, with neighbouring Pamunkey, Mattaponi, Chickahominy, Monacan, Nanticoke/Nentego and Powhatan-associated peoples in the wider Chesapeake/Potomac watershed. Sources state the Piscataway gained state recognition from Maryland in 2012, the Anacostan/Nacotchtank have no recognition to date, and the National Museum of the American Indian is cited for “no living Nacotchtank lineage” (survivors adopted into Piscataway). These are historical/cultural facts, not DC tribal land or a DC tribal cannabis programme. Sources: ALA — Indigenous Tribes of Washington, D.C.; APA land acknowledgement; The Eagle (2022) (all secondary, 24 October 2026).
  • Tribal reciprocity ≠ tribal operations in DC: ABCA's reciprocity list includes North Carolina (Eastern Band of Cherokee Indians) as a jurisdiction whose patient registrations DC retailers may accept (secondary list, 24 October 2026). That is a patient-card recognition rule for visitors from that tribal programme; it is not a tribal dispensary or grow in DC. ABCA's own patients page (index, 24 October 2026) says retailers may accept registrations from “most U.S. states, territories, and select tribal nations” and to confirm the jurisdiction is extended reciprocity before visiting. The full current reciprocity list should be taken from ABCA, not secondary lists — Gap C-21 (lists differ slightly between secondary sources: 38 jurisdictions in one summary, longer lists in others).
  • No DC tribal-state compact, tribal hemp plan, or tribal cannabis agreement was found. The federal baseline's tribal hemp-plan route (7 U.S.C. §1639p) has no DC application because there is no DC tribal government/land to plan for.

10.2 Interstate commerce, Maryland and Virginia

TopicPosition
Interstate commerce law / agreement None. No DC–Maryland, DC–Virginia or multi-state cannabis commerce agreement, compact or import/export programme exists. Transporting marijuana across a state/District line remains a federal offence (CSA) even where both sides have legalised some form, and DC's medical programme is an intra-District programme: licensees may only sell to qualifying patients/caregivers in DC, and cultivation/manufacturing must be DC-licensed. Mailing/shipping cannabis is likewise federal trafficking exposure, not a DC licensing question.
Reciprocity (the only cross-border patient mechanism) A visitor with a valid out-of-state medical registration from a reciprocity jurisdiction may purchase in DC; a DC registration does not authorise purchase in Maryland/Virginia or transport back home. Non-residents without a home card may take a temporary DC registration (3/30/90/180/365 days, $10/$20/$50/$75/$100 — Part 1 range $10–$100, confirmed by multiple secondary sources and ABCA patients page structure). DC resident registration is free, 2-year (Part 1 baseline).
Maryland (bordering, adult-use retail market since 1 Jul 2023) Secondary summary: adults 21+ may possess up to 1.5 oz flower (plus concentrate/THC caps), cultivate up to 2 plants per residence, buy only from Maryland-licensed retailers. Maryland law does not authorise bringing Maryland cannabis into DC for sale, nor DC cannabis into Maryland. Maryland's medical programme has been described (secondary, dated) as having reciprocity with neighbouring jurisdictions including DC — that description is outdated/secondary and should not be relied on for current Maryland reciprocity; check Maryland Cannabis Administration directly. Gap C-22.
Virginia (bordering, possession legal, no full adult-use retail system) Secondary summary: adults 21+ may possess up to 1 oz in public, share up to 1 oz without remuneration in private, cultivate up to 4 plants per household, but Virginia has no operating adult-use retail sales system; medical access is via Virginia's medical programme. Transport/import from other states is unlawful under Virginia law (secondary). Gap C-22 for current Virginia text.
Practical cross-border traps (1) Possession limits change at the line: DC 2 oz / MD 1.5 oz / VA 1 oz (public) — a DC-legal 2 oz amount exceeds Virginia's public limit. (2) Home-grow caps change: DC 6/household 12 vs MD 2 vs VA 4. (3) A DC-legal purchase becomes unlawful the moment it is carried into MD/VA for a non-medical purpose or in excess of that jurisdiction's limit, and is a federal offence in transit regardless. (4) Federal land inside DC (see Part 12.5) is the same trap without leaving the city. (5) Driving: see Part 12.2 — impairment law differs by jurisdiction; do not assume a DC registration is a defence in MD/VA.
What federal changes trigger (and do not trigger) in DC Schedule III split (28 Apr 2026, baseline): Does not repeal the Harris rider, does not create adult-use sales, does not amend DC Code. It changes the federal scheduling of state-licensed medical marijuana (and FDA-approved products) only. Any claim that it “opens a door for DC to act” on adult-use sales is analyst speculation (secondary, 2026) and is contradicted by the rider's continued presence in FY2026 (P.L. 119-75) — flag as VERIFY if a DC source asserts a trigger. Hemp redefinition (after 11 Dec 2026, baseline): Does not amend D.C. Code §48-901.02; DC's no-exemption rule persists until the Council acts. It may change what counts as lawful federal hemp in interstate transit, but it does not create a DC hemp licence or a DC retail right. 280E (baseline): Treasury's “generally removes 280E” position applies to businesses no longer dealing in Schedule I/II substances; adult-use remains fully subject. DC's own decoupling (D.C. Law 24-332, proviso VERIFY) is a separate DC tax question — Gap C-15. Federal medical rider through 11 Dec 2026 (baseline): Bars federal interference with state medical programmes in appropriations terms; whether it independently protects DC's programme (DC is not a state) was not verified from rider text — Gap C-14. No federal banking law enacted (baseline): DC licensees remain subject to federal banking constraints; no DC banking workaround was found in Run 2.

Part 11 — Operations rules

11.1 Testing

Legal trigger. D.C. Code §7-1671.05(8)–(11) requires, once at least one testing laboratory is licensed: cultivation centres segregate harvested cannabis into batches and hold from sale until tested, results received, and the centre determines the batch meets Board sale requirements; manufacturers do the same for processed products. Labs must report THC and CBD concentration, organic/inorganic status, fertiliser/nutrient presence/concentration, mould/mildew/pests, and mycotoxin/pesticide/heavy-metal (cultivation) or mycotoxin/residual-solvent (manufacturing) results against Board thresholds, plus any other Board-required information.

Source: codes.findlaw.com — D.C. Code §7-1671.05 (index, 24 October 2026); Chapter 16B text (opened).

Required tests (22-C DCMR Ch. 65, as summarised in ABCA rulemaking and secondary testing-lab guidance). A DC-licensed lab must be able to test for: microbial contamination; heavy metals (lead, mercury, cadmium, arsenic); water activity; moisture content; foreign matter; mycotoxin; residual solvents; homogeneity (edibles); pesticide and fertiliser residue; cannabinoid and terpene profile; and cannabinoid potency at minimum for CBDA, THCA, CBN, CBD and delta-9 THC.

Sources: ABCA testing-lab application instructions (Mar 2026 PDF, via search result: abca.dc.gov — Testing Lab Application Instructions, March 2026 (PDF)) and secondary summary WashingtonDCCannabis.org/testing-lab (index, 24 October 2026). The current 22-C Ch. 65 thresholds table itself was not opened line-by-line in Run 2 — Gap C-23: open 22-C DCMR §§6500–6511 and the ABCA testing-lab application for numeric limits, sampling sizes and methods.

Lab accreditation / qualifications. Testing-lab applicants must show: OECD GLP principles compliance; current ISO/IEC 17025 accreditation; internal SOPs; a QA/QC programme; and adherence to the American Herbal Products Association Recommendations for Regulators — Cannabis Operations (current version), plus a laboratory testing plan (knowledge, experience, training, certifications, QA/QC, proficiency testing, analytical processes, chain of custody, sample retention, space, recordkeeping, results reporting, corrective action, accreditation timeline) and an environmental plan (see Part 9.1, DOEE). (ABCA testing-lab application instructions, Mar 2026, via search result; verify against the current ABCA PDF — the search-result PDF text is from the application instructions, not the regulation itself.)

Sampling / reporting. Test results must be uploaded into ABCA's electronic tracking system (METRC — Part 1 baseline, seed-to-sale system named by official ABCA sources). Secondary guidance states a lab must upload a result within 24 hours of the test (citing 22-C §6410 in older numbering; current numbering may differ after the 2023–2025 rulemakings — Gap C-23). Labs may not test product not obtained from a licensed medical cannabis business and not identified in the real-time electronic records system, and may not endorse/advertise on behalf of a cultivator/dispensary/brand/strain (older 22-C §6401.14–.16, via ABCA 2023 rulemaking PDF). Patient samples: §7-1671.05(11A) allows labs to test a registered patient's product if purchased from a licensed DC retailer/internet retailer, and to test cultivator/manufacturer samples for QA/R&D (non-random selection allowed for QA/R&D, but QA/R&D tests do not satisfy the mandatory batch-testing requirements).

Source: D.C. Law 25-255 text (index, 24 October 2026) for §7-1671.05(11A); ABCA 2023 rulemaking PDF abca.dc.gov — Medical Cannabis Emergency and Proposed Rulemaking, 26 Apr 2023 (PDF)

Failed tests, remediation and recalls

  • Remediation (final rulemaking, Board adopted 3 Dec 2025; deemed Council-approved 19 Nov 2025; effective 5 days after DC Register publication): 22-C §6510 (numbered §6511 in the emergency drafts): if a sample of cannabis oil or dried/unprocessed plant material fails microbiological, mycotoxin, heavy-metal or residual-solvent tests, the business may remediate the batch and must submit the remediated batch for retesting; if it passes the failed test and all other applicable tests, the prior failure does not require destruction. If dried/unprocessed material fails retesting, it may be processed into cannabis oil (unless the failure was due to a §5620.1 violation), and that oil is then tested/remediated under the same section.
  • No remediation for pesticides:

“A medical cannabis business may not remediate or process into cannabis oil any cannabis or cannabis product where a sample … fails a test related to the presence of pesticides or … was produced or grown in violation of §5620.1.” (§6510.4)

Source (opened, 24 October 2026): abca.dc.gov — Medical Cannabis Remediation-Testing Final Rulemaking, Dec 2025 (PDF)
  • Quarantine / destruction (secondary summary, to verify against Ch. 65): A batch that fails must be immediately quarantined until reprocessed/re-tested or destroyed; the lab reports failed results within 24 hours of complete analysis. A licensee's own operating plan (example filed in a zoning case) describes recall as: on learning of a manufacturer/cultivator recall, the retailer uses METRC to identify every qualifying patient who purchased that product and calls each patient to inform them. That is an applicant SOP example, not a quoted regulation — a DC-wide mandatory recall-notification rule (who must notify, in what time, to ABCA vs patients) was not found as a regulation in Run 2. Gap C-24: check 22-C Ch. 65/66 for a recall section and any ABCA recall notices.
  • No public DC recall list was located in Run 2. No ABCA “recall” page or press release recalling a specific batch was returned. Do not invent a recall history.

Certified / accredited labs — list

LabStatusSource
ClearSight Labs First DC medical cannabis testing laboratory licence, issued 16 August 2024; testing requirement triggered on that date. ABCA notice abca.dc.gov — Testing Laboratory Notice (index, 24 October 2026)
Any second lab? Not confirmed. Secondary market summaries (Q3 2026) still describe ClearSight as “the licensed testing lab” (singular) and ABCA August 2026 totals in secondary summaries list “1 testing lab.” Run 2 did not open ABCA's current licensed-business list to confirm whether a second lab has since been licensed. Gap C-25: open ABCA's current licensed cultivation/manufacturer/testing list (abca.dc.gov medical cannabis business pages) and confirm the lab count.

11.2 Licence rules — transfers, ownership, renewal, caps, relocation, surrender/revocation, insurance/bonding

Transfers / sales / ownership changes

  • A licence is a revocable privilege, not a property right, and cannot be sold, assigned, transferred, pledged or otherwise encumbered without prior Board approval (secondary legal summary citing §7-1671.06(j) and 22-C §§5413, 5501.5, 6401.1–.2 — verify against current Code/DCMR text; Gap C-26).
  • 22-C §5402.1 (2023 rulemaking PDF): a person applying for issuance, transfer to a new owner, or renewal of a licence/endorsement/permit, or for approval of substantial changes including transfer to a new location, must file a Board-prescribed application.
  • Ownership disclosure / approval thresholds (secondary legal summary — VERIFY against 22-C §§5501–5502): all ownership/control interests must be disclosed; a change in majority ownership or controlling interest requires prior Board approval; a change in equity holders owning 1% or more requires disclosure of all required information on the new holder to ABCA within 15 calendar days. Debt that grants control/equity rights or is secured by the licence requires pre-approval; routine term loans/lines without conversion rights typically require disclosure only. M&A requires Board approval before any change of ownership/control takes effect.
  • Live examples (Sep 2026 Board agenda, secondary report of Board action 16 Sep 2026): transfer of a social-equity internet-retailer application (ZMTS LLC / Gifted Curators → The Cannopy LLC, 1922 I St NW); transfer of a social-equity cultivation application (Maven Wellness Group → Noble Farms LLC, 2060 West Virginia Ave NE); transfer of a Type 1 manufacturer application (Elevated Holdings / Adose Wellness → Luma LLC, 2149 Queens Chapel Rd NE, ownership unchanged). These show transfers are routinely considered but are Board actions, not private sales. Source: The Marijuana Herald (secondary, 24 October 2026)
  • Social-equity transfer lock: A licence issued to a social-equity applicant cannot be transferred to a new owner until 3 years after issuance unless transferred to another social-equity applicant. After 3 years, transfer to a non-social-equity / non-CBE owner requires repayment of any District grants/loans and any waived licensing/application fees. (ABCA guidance summaries, Mondaq/Outlaw Report, 24 October 2026 — verify against §7-1671.06 text; Gap C-26.)
  • Straw ownership is expressly prohibited (§7-1671.01(20D), opened 24 October 2026).

Renewal requirements / timelines

  • Business licences: The current 22-C renewal timeline, renewal fee schedule and late-renewal grace period were not stated in a regulation opened in Run 2. What is documented: (a) failure to renew/pay annual fees leads to a cease-and-desist and then cancellation — e.g. Board Order cancelling UND Necessities LLC t/a DC Smoke, Retailer Licence ABRA-126787, 4 Mar 2026, after a 28 Jan 2026 cease-and-desist for failure to renew/pay annual fees, with METRC access cancelled (abca.dc.gov — DC Smoke Board Order (PDF)); (b) conditional licences: originally 1 year to commence operations, extended — a 2025 emergency bill (B26-0344, secondary BillTrack summary) would convert 1-year conditional licences in effect 17 Dec 2024 to 3-year conditional licences; JD Supra (2025) states conditional licensees initially had 1 year, “extended to up to three years.” Whether a permanent 3-year conditional term is now in the Code is VERIFY — Gap C-27 (check current §7-1671.06 and ABCA conditional-licence pages; do not rely on an emergency bill summary).
  • Patient registrations (for contrast, Part 1 baseline confirmed): DC resident 2-year, free (digital); physical card $10; non-resident 3–365 days, $10–$100. ABCA's new portal note (Aug 2026): registrations submitted in the legacy system before 8:00 a.m. 18 Aug 2026 still process there and do not transfer to the new portal — a practical renewal trap. Source: abca.dc.gov — Medical Cannabis Patients (index, 24 October 2026).
  • Employee registration: ABCA employee applications require headshot photo, unexpired government photo ID, manager training certificate (managers only), background check (owners/agents). Source: abca.dc.gov — Medical Cannabis Employee Application (index, 24 October 2026).

Ownership caps (§7-1671.06(e)(1), current Code text via search result — verify against D.C. Law Library current section)

  • Testing-lab licensee: may not hold a cultivation, manufacturer, retailer, internet-retailer or courier licence.
  • Any retailer/internet-retailer/cultivation/testing/manufacturer licensee: may not hold a courier licence.
  • No licensee may hold more than 2 cultivation centre licences.
  • Combined retailer + internet-retailer licences per licensee: ≤3.
  • No limit on the number of manufacturer licences.
  • Cultivation licensee: ≤1 retailer licence; may not hold an internet-retailer licence.
  • The Board may modify these numbers by rule (§7-1671.06(e)(2)).

Source: code.dccouncil.gov — D.C. Code §7-1671.06 (index, 24 October 2026). Note: an older engrossed-bill version said “one retailer or internet retailer” for cultivators; the current text as reproduced separates retailer (≤1) and internet retailer (prohibited) — use the current text, and flag the older wording as superseded.

Relocation

ABCA has a dedicated Medical Cannabis Change of Location application: permits a licensee to apply for a change of location or expansion into an adjacent property, subject to ABC Board approval. A cultivation/manufacturer holder that owns or validly leases adjacent real property may be approved to physically expand into it (§7-1671.06(f)). Relocation must still satisfy zoning buffers (Part 1 baseline: 400 ft / 300 ft grandfathered), ANC notice, and — for retailers — the Board's “great weight” ANC rule and protest process.

Source: abca.dc.gov — Medical Cannabis Change of Location (index, 24 October 2026); §7-1671.06(f) via search result.

Surrender / safekeeping / revocation

  • Safekeeping: A licensee that closes/ceases operation must immediately notify the Board in writing and surrender the licence for safekeeping (Board order language, 2024). The licence “enters Safekeeping once the Safekeeping application has been approved.” Operating with an inactive/safekeeping licence is a violation (Board order sustaining a charge for delay in physically surrendering the licence after approval 25 Oct 2023–31 Mar 2024, with a $30,000 fine + 30-day suspension as a fourth primary-tier offence in four years — alcohol-licence tier language in that order; apply the cannabis safekeeping principle, not the fine tier, without verification). Sep 2026 Board agenda included three original safekeeping requests (SK Quality Services / Blunt DC; Pot & Goods LLC; Power of Plants / Mr. Nice Guys — all with no location listed).
  • Summary suspension/revocation (22-C Ch. 62, 2023 rulemaking PDF): Notice must state operations must cease immediately (except necessary tending by cultivation centres), require immediate ABCA inventory, require surrender of all registration cards/permits within 24 hours, state reasons/violations, and state the right to request a hearing within 3 business days; the Board must hold a hearing within 2 business days of a timely request and decide within 3 business days after the hearing. Appeal lies to the DC Court of Appeals under the DC Administrative Procedure Act.
  • General revocation/suspension power: §7-1671.05(13) — the Board may revoke/suspend any registration/licence for violation of Chapter 16B or its rules (Chapter 16B text, opened).
  • Unlicensed-establishment closure: see Parts 9.2 and 12.1 (closure orders, padlock, remediation plan before return of premises, $10,000 owner fine authority in the Jan 2025 example — the general fine scale in Law 25-255 should be quoted from the statute, Gap C-17).

Insurance / bonding minimums — NOT FOUND as a numeric rule

No DC regulation stating a minimum general-liability, product-liability or surety-bond amount for medical cannabis licensees was located in Run 2. The only insurance item found is procedural: a cultivation-centre transport permit application requires a copy of vehicle insurance (amount not stated). ABCA application checklists may require proof of insurance as a general business matter, but a dollar minimum cannot be stated from Run 2 sources. Gap C-28: check ABCA business-licence application instructions/checklists (all licence types) and 22-C Ch. 54 for an insurance/bond section before advising any client on coverage. Do not import another state's bond (e.g. $25,000 / [Removed — fine is per D.C. Code §22-3571.01, amount not stated here] figures common elsewhere) into DC advice.

11.3 Cultivation rules

Commercial (licensed cultivation centres)

  • Canopy tiers (Part 4 baseline, confirmed by multiple secondary sources in Run 2): Tier 1: >0–10,000 sq ft mature canopy; Tier 2: >10,000–25,000; Tier 3: >25,000–50,000; Tier 4: >50,000–75,000; Tier 5: >75,000–100,000; Tier 6: >100,000 sq ft. Annual fees $11,000–$36,000 standard (Part 4 baseline), 25% for social equity first 3 years (i.e. 75% reduction — same figure, two framings). Sources: mycannabis.com; washingtondccannabis.org/licensing/cultivation; canndelta.com (all index, 24 October 2026). The tier definitions should be verified against current §7-1671.06 / 22-C — Gap C-29 (secondary sources agree, but no regulation text was opened for the tier table).
  • Plant counts (commercial): No commercial plant-count cap (as opposed to canopy) was found in Run 2. Older, unreliable secondary pages claim “[Removed — no such DC rule found] / [Removed — no such DC rule found]” for DC cultivation — those figures describe neither the current tier system nor home cultivation and should be disregarded. Gap C-29.
  • Indoor / outdoor (commercial): No explicit indoor-only or outdoor-permission rule for licensed cultivation centres was located in a regulation opened in Run 2. Zoning (non-residential district, buffers), visibility (product/paraphernalia not visible from public/other property, older 22-C §5710.1), security, odour/air and building-code constraints in practice favour indoor cultivation, but an outdoor permission/prohibition cannot be stated. Gap C-30: check 22-C Ch. 56 (cultivation centres) and DOB zoning use permissions for outdoor cultivation.
  • Home cultivation (for contrast, Part 1 baseline + Code): Inside the principal residence only, ≤6 plants (≤3 mature), household aggregate ≤12 (≤6 mature); plants and product must stay inside the home, out of public view and inaccessible to under-21s (secondary MPD summary). Outdoor home grows are not permitted (secondary, consistent across sources). Medical registration does not expand home-grow limits (secondary, Safe Access Now).

Cultivation-centre operational duties (from §7-1671.05 and 22-C summaries)

  • Batch segregation + hold-for-testing (see §11.1); real-time electronic records of every transaction, quantity on hand, and disposal method/evidence (§7-1671.05(6)); notify ABCA and MPD Chief in writing within 24 hours of loss/theft/destruction; no processing/transfer of products likely to appeal to minors (shape/design/flavour modelled on children's products, animal/vehicle/person/character shapes), no cannabinoid concentrates applied to commercially available candy/snack items, no DMSO-containing products (older 22-C §5608.5, 2017 DOH compilation — verify current numbering; Gap C-23).
  • Pesticides: see §11.6.

11.4 Security, storage and transport

Security (older 22-C §5610, 2017/2018 DOH compilations — verify current numbering, Gap C-23; substance still cited in ABCA security-plan requirements)

  • 24/7 CCTV in good working order covering all building entrances/exits, parking lots, rear alleys immediately adjacent, and the entire interior including all limited-access areas and all areas where cannabis is cultivated, stored, dispensed or destroyed; fixed cameras, maximised facial/body image quality, no backlighting/obstructions; outdoor/low-light cameras must be day/night, minimum 600 lines/inch (analog) or D1 (IP), minimum 0.7 LUX; digital video recorder meeting the (truncated in source) standard.
  • Safe-use facilities: cameras operable and able to record for a minimum of 30 days (§7-1671.06c(b)(1), opened via Code section page).
  • Retailer security plan (ABCA application): must show 22-C compliance, a site plan (streets, parking, other tenants, bordering entities), a floor plan marking entrances/exits, every camera and field of view, skylights/windows/roof hatches, alarm inputs (motion detectors, door contacts, duress/hold-up devices), alarm sirens, video recorder and alarm-panel locations, restricted vs public areas; and security training for personnel including violent-incident/emergency procedures and calling MPD, plus camera numbers/locations. Source: washingtondccannabis.org/licensing/retailer (secondary summary of ABCA requirements, 24 October 2026).
  • Exchange capture: the entire retailer–patient/caregiver exchange must be clearly captured on video surveillance; curbside/at-the-door pickup only in view of cameras, otherwise not permitted; quick/efficient pickup procedures and a patient receipt recordkeeping mechanism (22-C §5703, 2023 rulemaking PDF).
  • Storage: Product must be stored in secure/limited-access areas covered by CCTV (above); retailers must segregate expired/unusable/adulterated/contaminated product into a segregated safe pending disposal (applicant SOP example, Part 11.5); safe-use facilities must destroy unadministered product left abandoned/unclaimed in the consumption area (§7-1671.06c(b)(3)).
  • Alarms: Alarm inputs/sirens are part of the required security plan (above); a specific “alarm must transmit to a monitoring station” rule was stated only in an unreliable secondary page and is not relied on here — Gap C-31.

Transport

  • Licensee-to-licensee transport: 22-C §5621 (2023 rulemaking): a licensed medical cannabis business must obtain a transporter licence from the Board to transport medical cannabis within DC to another licensed business if permitted by law; an original transporter licence is required for each vehicle designated to deliver to another licensed business. A cultivation centre may not deliver to any premises other than the specific licensed premises of the internet retailer/retailer where the product is to be sold for resale (22-C §5703.6, 2023 rulemaking).
  • Cultivation-centre transport permits: ABCA form requires vehicle insurance copy and a signed/notarised Transportation Permit Acknowledgement & Attestation Form; the permit registers vehicles, not drivers; drivers must be registered employees; contracted delivery services must register as contracted agents and their drivers as employees. Source: abca.dc.gov — Transport Permit Instructions 2025 (PDF) (index, 24 October 2026).
  • Patient/caregiver transport: in a labelled container or sealed package, in the manner/method established by rulemaking (§7-1671.03(c), opened via Code text).
  • Courier delivery to patients: via licensed couriers under contract to retailers/internet retailers (Part 4 licence type, $8,000); delivery endorsement for retailers is $300 (Part 4 baseline). No DC courier may hold any other medical licence (§7-1671.06(e)(1)(B)).
  • Interstate transport: prohibited as federal trafficking (Part 10.2); no DC transport licence authorises it.

11.5 Waste disposal

  • Statutory record duty: Licensees must maintain real-time electronic records of “the disposal method used for any medical cannabis that was cultivated, processed, or acquired but did not meet the requirements for sale … or that was not sold for any other reason, including evidence of the disposal” (§7-1671.05(6)(A)(iv), Chapter 16B text).
  • No standalone DC “render unusable by 50% mixing / grinding” regulation was located in Run 2. The 50%-mixing rule quoted in many web summaries belongs to other states (e.g. Mississippi, Nevada-style rules found in search) and must not be attributed to DC. Applicant SOP examples filed in DC zoning cases describe the practical DC workflow: daily + monthly inventory audits for expired/unusable/adulterated/contaminated product → transfer from the active-inventory safe to a segregated-products safe → update METRC → coordinate with MPD to pick up the segregated product for disposal, with MPD certification of relinquishment for destruction/disposal. That SOP is an applicant's plan, not a quoted DCMR section.
  • Adulterated product: “No licensee shall distribute, sell, or transfer adulterated medical cannabis to another licensee or person unless the product is being sent to a testing laboratory … for disposal or destruction, complying with a court order, law enforcement investigation, or order of the Board.” (22-C §6500.3, 2023 rulemaking summary.)
  • Safe-use waste: destroy all unadministered cannabis left abandoned/unclaimed in the designated consumption area (§7-1671.06c(b)(3)).
  • Gap C-32: Open 22-C Ch. 56/65 for a dedicated waste/destruction section (method, witnessing, MPD role, time limits) before advising on a waste SOP. Do not cite a 50% rule for DC.

11.6 Environmental — water, pesticides, energy

TopicWhat was foundWhat was NOT found
Pesticides 22-C §5620.5 (2023 rulemaking): Pesticides may legally be used on medical cannabis by cultivation centres only if: (a) registered with DOEE; (b) use complies with DOEE regulations; (c) used per label/labelling, and for EPA §3 (FIFRA) products only if all active ingredients are exempt from tolerance under 40 CFR Part 180 Subparts D/E, the label allows the intended site, the label expressly allows use on crops/plants intended for human consumption, and the active ingredients are allowed for use on tobacco by EPA. (A later rulemaking note deletes §§5620.6(c), 5620.7, 5620.8 on pesticide recommendations/lists as non-enforceable policy — the §5620.5 criteria above remain the operative test in the 2023 text; verify current numbering.) Pesticide failure = no remediation (§6510.4, Part 11.1). “Pesticide” is defined in §7-1671.01(14A) (opened). A published DOEE “approved for cannabis” pesticide list was not located in Run 2 — the rulemaking note suggests DOEE does not publish such a list as a regulation. Gap C-33.
Water Testing-lab applicants must address wastewater/runoff treatment in their environmental plan (ABCA testing-lab instructions). No numeric water-use, discharge-permit or runoff rule for cultivation centres was opened. Secondary cultivation guides claim DOEE water-record / runoff / wastewater-permit duties — not verified against a DOEE regulation. Gap C-34: check DOEE water / wastewater discharge rules as applied to cultivation/manufacturing.
Energy Testing-lab environmental plan must address minimising carbon footprint/resource needs and using alternative energy. No DC energy-use cap, efficiency mandate or reporting rule for cultivation centres was found. Gap C-34.
Air / odour Safe-use facilities with combustible products must have ventilation directing air outside through a pollution/odour-control unit that eliminates all detectable odour, smoke and combustion by-products so as to prevent any public nuisance (§7-1671.06c(c)(3), Code text). Testing-lab plans must address scrubbing/treating exchanged air. General odour/nuisance enforcement for cultivation/retail premises was not quantified. Gap C-34.
General environmental plans Cultivation/manufacturer/retailer applicants file operating/environmental plans as part of licensing (ABCA checklists); the testing-lab environmental plan is the most detailed example found. Gap C-34.

11.7 Sampling, giveaways, discounts, promotions — and the lawful-gifting line

The line (the single most important DC consumer rule)

  • Lawful gift (Initiative 71 / §48-904.01(a)(1)(B)): An adult 21+ may transfer ≤1 oz to another adult 21+ without remuneration. “Without remuneration” means no money, goods or services in exchange.
  • Unlawful sale (same section, §48-904.01(a)(1)(D) proviso): “nothing in this subsection shall make it lawful to sell, offer for sale, or make available for sale any marijuana or cannabis plants.” DC courts (Kornegay v. United States, 236 A.3d 414, 420 (D.C. 2020), cited in an ABCA filing) read §48-904.01(a)(1) to permit possession of ≤2 oz “so long as that adult does not ‘sell, offer for sale, or make available for sale’ the marijuana.”
  • Disguised sale = sale: Selling a sticker, cookie, art, T-shirt, “consultation” or delivery fee with cannabis as a “free gift” is treated as an unlicensed commercial transaction, not a gift. That is the entire basis for the gifting-shop crackdown (Parts 9.2, 12.1). The 2024 law also makes it unlawful for any licensed or unlicensed business to represent its operations as “I-71 compliant” (Vicente LLP summary of the 2024 Act, 24 October 2026).
  • Lawful purchase: Only from a licensed medical retailer/internet retailer (via courier/delivery where endorsed), by a registered patient/caregiver (including reciprocity/temporary registrants), within the 8 oz / 30-day dispensing cap (Part 1 baseline; ABCA patients page confirms “up to 8 ounces … within a rolling 30-day period”).

Sampling

  • Patient/consumer sampling at retail: No general “free sample” right for unregistered consumers exists. Within the medical programme, education tasting is a retailer endorsement ($130, Part 4 baseline) — its detailed conditions (quantities, on-premises consumption, product forms) were not opened in a regulation in Run 2. Safe-use facilities allow administration on the premises of product purchased at that retailer at the time of purchase (see Part 12.4) — that is not a free-sample programme.
  • QA/R&D sampling: Labs may test cultivator/manufacturer QA/R&D samples, non-randomly selected, but those tests do not satisfy mandatory batch testing (§7-1671.05(11A)(B)).
  • Giveaways / contests: Safe-use endorsement holders may not “encourage or permit an organized game or contest on the premises that involves consuming cannabis or cannabis products or the awarding of cannabis or cannabis products” (§7-1671.06c(d)(5)). By extension, a “win free cannabis” promotion at a licensed retailer should be treated as prohibited until ABCA confirms otherwise — Gap C-35 for a general giveaway rule outside safe-use premises.

Discounts / promotions

  • Discounts are allowed in the medical programme (secondary advertising summaries list “Discounts / Promotions: Allowed”), and retailers in practice advertise new-patient (e.g. 20% off), veteran (10%) and senior (5%) discounts and points programmes (retailer sites, secondary). No DC rule prohibiting price discounts was found.
  • Sliding-scale discount programme (22-C Ch. 98, as amended 2023): The 2023 rulemaking “modifies the sliding scale discount program in Chapter 98 to eliminate the requirement to dedicate 2 percent of gross revenue, require the provision of the discount to all qualified patients that qualify, eliminate revenue reporting requirements, and create an affirmative defense for failing to provide the discount if provision would otherwise violate the law.” Who “qualifies” for the sliding-scale discount (income/Medicaid-type criteria) and the discount percentage were not stated in the rulemaking summary opened — Gap C-36: open 22-C Ch. 98.
  • Tax holiday (context, not a discount rule): DC law establishes an annual medical cannabis sales-tax holiday around 20 April (6% waived at licensed retailers, excluding paraphernalia, for registered patients); 2024 ran 15–28 Apr. Whether it ran in 2025/2026 was not verified in Run 2 (secondary market report flags this as unverified) — Gap C-37.
  • Advertising / signage (22-C Ch. 58, as amended Feb 2025, 4th Emergency Rulemaking): Exterior signs advertising medical cannabis may not in aggregate exceed 10 sq ft (trade-name signage on the building exterior excepted); signs indicating cannabis is on the property (other than trade name) may only be displayed in the interior window and occupy ≤25% of window space; no such sign on the exterior or interior of any door; non-conforming signs must be removed. Older §5800 rules (still cited in secondary summaries): price advertisements not in windows; no medical-cannabis advertising on the exterior of any window/door; no illuminated sign advertising medical cannabis visible from outside. Prohibited statements (§5801, older text): no picture/illustration depicting a child/immature person or child-suggestive objects (e.g. toys), no design especially appealing to children, no statement known to be false/misleading, and no encouragement of purchase/use by persons without a District registration (secondary summaries). Unlicensed establishments are prohibited from displaying signage/visual advertising related to cannabis products and prices (2024 Act, Vicente summary). Sources: abca.dc.gov — Signs and Advertising Fourth Rulemaking, Feb 2025 (PDF); mygrasslands.com (secondary, 24 October 2026)
  • Tie-in purchases prohibited: A cultivator/manufacturer may not require a licensee to purchase one product in order to purchase another; a retailer/internet retailer may not require a patient/caregiver to purchase one product in order to purchase another (22-C §5706, 2023 rulemaking).

11.8 Drug testing — licence holders/staff and DC government employees

Licence-holder staff

  • No DC regulation was found in Run 2 imposing routine drug testing on licensed medical cannabis business employees as a licence condition. Employees must be 21+ (applicant SOP example), registered with ABCA (employee application, Part 11.2), and managers must hold ABCA-approved medical cannabis training (Board Order 2026-211 required owner/staff training within 60 days as a sanction condition — a case-specific order, not a general testing rule).
  • Employers in DC generally: Cannabis Employment Protection Amendment Act of 2022 (D.C. Code §32-931 et seq. in secondary summaries) — an employer may test a prospective employee for marijuana only after a conditional offer; the Act prohibits adverse action based solely on cannabis use, with exceptions for (a) safety-sensitive positions (where impairment could foreseeably cause serious bodily injury/death), (b) employers required by federal statute/regulation/contract/funding agreement to prohibit cannabis use, and (c) use/possession during work hours or at work. It does not require employers to permit use/possession at work or during work hours, and does not protect impairment on the job. Civil penalties for violations are summarised secondarily as up to $1,000 (1–30 employees) / $2,500 (31–99) / $5,000 (100+) per violation, enforceable via the Office of Human Rights with a private right of action — verify against current §32-931 text; Gap C-38. Medical patients: employers should treat medical use for disabilities like other prescribed medication, with a narrow non-smokable-form-at-work accommodation described secondarily for non-safety-sensitive roles where no federal conflict exists — also Gap C-38.

DC government employees (DCHR / E-DPM — official guidance, index, 24 October 2026)

  • Initiative 71 has “no legal effect or impact” on DC government drug-and-alcohol testing programmes. Source: edpm.dc.gov — Marijuana and the District's Drug & Alcohol Testing
  • Medical marijuana (DCHR): A DC government employee authorised by a licensed physician to use marijuana medicinally may do so per the laws of their state of residence, provided use does not impair or impede safe performance of duties. Safety-sensitive employees remain subject to random screening; on selection they must comply, but may disclose programme participation via three routes: (1) submit the testing order + valid registration card to DCHR immediately before/after screening; (2) inform the Medical Review Officer (MRO) on a positive result; (3) if neither occurs and a termination notice issues, supply the registration card + written explanation to the Hearing Officer.
  • Reasonable suspicion (DCHR): A positive cannabis result after a properly referred reasonable-suspicion test constitutes evidence of impermissible impairment, regardless of medical programme participation. The employee may rebut with clear and convincing evidence (e.g. CBD-only with trace THC, or use so remote in time as to present no impairment risk). Safety-sensitive employees who test positive are presumed in violation. Supervisors must complete reasonable-suspicion training within 60 days of appointment and at least every 2 years. Source: edpm.dc.gov — Reasonable Suspicion Drug or Alcohol Impairment (index, 24 October 2026).
  • Safety-sensitive testing method (DCHR): Urinalysis detecting THC, per federal guidelines at 49 CFR Part 40 (protocol reference). Source: edpm.dc.gov — Marijuana and Safety-Sensitive Employees (index, 24 October 2026).
  • Federal employees / federal contractors in DC remain under federal drug-free workplace / testing rules; DC law does not protect them. (General principle; specific agency rules not researched in Run 2.)
  • Earlier patient-employment protection (historical): The Medical Marijuana Program Patient Employment Protection Temporary Amendment Act (Council, unanimous, ~2019–2020, secondary Merry Jane report) barred DC government agencies from firing/refusing to hire/penalising a registered patient based on patient status or a positive test unless reasonable suspicion of impairment at work/during hours existed, except safety-sensitive roles and federally required testing. Its relationship to the permanent 2022 Cannabis Employment Protection Act (above) was not traced in Run 2 — Gap C-38.

11.9 Research and university cannabis licences

  • No DC “research licence” category exists in Chapter 16B or the ABCA licence list. The licence types are cultivation, manufacturer, retailer, internet retailer, courier and testing laboratory (plus certification-training provider permit, Part 4 context). A university that wanted to cultivate/manufacture/test cannabis in DC would need the corresponding medical licence (and, for Schedule I/III material, federal DEA/FDA compliance) — no university-specific pathway was found.
  • What universities actually do in DC (secondary, not a licence): Georgetown University Medical Center (GUMC) research on cannabinoid signalling/development (no funding sought for that study, per GUMC page); Howard University Division of Pediatrics / Child Health study (with Rana Pediatric Fund) on “Perceived Effects of Cannabis Use Among Adolescents and Young Adults with Sickle Cell Disease” — a survey/perceived-effects study, not a DC cultivation or dispensing programme. General secondary claims that “Georgetown and Howard contribute to the evidence base” are not licence evidence. Sources: gumc.georgetown.edu; bakersfieldblackmagazine.com (secondary, 24 October 2026)
  • Testing-lab QA/R&D (Part 11.1) is the closest DC-law research mechanism: licensed labs may test cultivator/manufacturer QA/R&D samples, and cultivators/manufacturers may hold product for QA/R&D testing — but QA/R&D tests do not satisfy mandatory batch testing.
  • Gap C-39: Check ABCA for any “research” endorsement, DEA Schedule I/III researcher registration as applied to DC universities, and any DC university DEA licence — none was found in Run 2 searches.

Part 12 — Consumer and social rules

12.1 Paraphernalia and smoke shops — including gifting-shop enforcement and penalties

Paraphernalia — lawful

D.C. Code §48-1103(b)(1) (Chapter 11, Drug Paraphernalia): Except as authorised by Chapter 16B, it is unlawful to deliver/sell, possess with intent to deliver/sell, or manufacture with intent to deliver/sell drug paraphernalia knowing (or where one reasonably should know) it will be used to plant/propagate/cultivate/grow/harvest/manufacture/compound/convert/produce/process/prepare/test/analyse/ pack/repack/store/contain/conceal/inject/ingest/inhale or otherwise introduce a controlled substance into the body — except it is lawful to do so where one knows/has reason to know the paraphernalia will be used solely for marijuana use lawful under §48-904.01(a) or solely for growing/possession/harvesting/processing of cannabis plants lawful under §48-904.01(a).

Source: preview.code.dccouncil.gov — Title 48, Chapter 11; codes.findlaw.com — §48-1103 (index, 24 October 2026).

  • Penalties (same section): violation = up to 6 months imprisonment and/or a fine per §22-3571.01, or up to 2 years if after a prior DC conviction under the subchapter; delivery by a person 18+ to a person under 18 who is at least 3 years younger = special offence, up to 8 years; where a commercial retail/wholesale establishment sells paraphernalia in violation, the court shall revoke the licensee's licence and the certificate of occupancy for the premises.
  • Community-based organisations (§7-404(a)(1)) and DC government employees/contractors/grantees acting within scope may deliver/possess paraphernalia for personal use of a controlled substance notwithstanding §48-1103(b)(1) (§48-1103(b)(1A)–(1B)).
  • Medical programme definition: “Paraphernalia” in §7-1671.01(14) covers objects/kits/devices/equipment for preparing, storing, ingesting, inhaling or otherwise introducing medical cannabis, and for planting/propagating/manufacturing/cultivating/growing/harvesting/processing/preparing medical cannabis (opened, 24 October 2026). Licensed retailers may manufacture, purchase and sell paraphernalia (Part 4 baseline).
  • Practical result: pipes, rolling papers, bongs, vaporizers and grow equipment for lawful adult/medical use are lawful to possess and sell in DC. The same item sold knowing it will be used for unlawful controlled-substance use is not.

Smoke shops / tobacco shops — no separate “smoke shop cannabis licence”

  • A tobacco/smoke shop has no cannabis-sale right by virtue of selling tobacco or paraphernalia. Multiple padlocked businesses in ABCA enforcement are literally tobacco/smoke shops that sold cannabis without a licence: District Tobacco Shop, 2626 Georgia Ave NW, and Green Department, 2720 Georgia Ave NW (padlocked 15–16 Jan 2025; >5 lbs marijuana, 105 g THC hookah filling, ~2 lbs THC edibles, 2 g THC oil at the former; 8.9 lbs at the latter); Potomac Tobacco, 1300–1302 Pennsylvania Ave SE (30 Jan 2026; 1.7 oz marijuana, 300 g pre-rolls, 149 g THC vape cartridges; one arrest). A shop that sells only lawful paraphernalia/tobacco and no cannabis is not an “unlicensed cannabis establishment”; a shop that sells/gifts cannabis with a purchase is.
  • No DC “smoke shop licence” that authorises cannabis sales was found; do not advise a client to open a “smoke shop” as a cannabis workaround.

Gifting-shop enforcement and penalties (consolidated)

  • Civil (Board/ABCA, Law 25-255 / 2024 emergency acts): Closure orders, padlocking, seizure/disposal of contraband, fines for unlicensed establishments (secondary scale: $10,000 first / $20,000 second / $30,000 third — verify against Law 25-255 §7-1671.08(f) text, Gap C-17); fines for commercial property owners who fail to prevent unlawful cannabis activity on their property (Jan 2024 law: “more than $10K” per Bisnow; Jan 2025 ABCA example: Board “has the authority to issue a $10,000 fine to each property owner”; owners may be required to submit a Board-approved remediation plan before premises are returned); prohibition on “I-71 compliant” representations and on unlicensed cannabis signage/price advertising; inspections (announced and unannounced) of unlicensed establishments (§7-1671.05(14A)).
  • Criminal (DC Code, for unlicensed sale/distribution): Secondary penalty summaries state unlicensed distribution = felony, up to 5 years / [Removed — fine is per D.C. Code §22-3571.01, amount not stated here], enhanced near schools / involving minors; possession >2 oz (non-patient, knowing/intentional) = misdemeanour up to 6 months / $1,000. These summaries are secondary — quote penalties from §§48-904.01, 48-904.02 et seq. directly before advising (Gap C-40).
  • Licensed-business sanctions (examples, 2026): Doobie District (KLM LLC, 1526 U St NW), Board Order No. 2026-211 (11 Feb 2026): 30-day suspension for dispensing to non-qualified patients and entering false METRC information (undercover purchases without ID/registration check; labels bearing an employee's name/patient number; employee account oversold beyond the 8 oz / 30-day limit), plus ABCA-approved training for owners/staff within 60 days. Source: Outlaw Report (secondary report of Board order, 24 October 2026)
  • Festival / pop-up enforcement: Kosmic Treats, H Street Festival, Sep 2026 — Board unanimous cease-and-desist after undercover purchase of a pre-roll and THC gummies; unlicensed operation designated a public nuisance; emergency shutdown before a formal hearing on “irreparable harm” grounds. Source: Outlaw Report (secondary, Oct 2026)
  • Cumulative closures (reconciliation): ABCA: 19 (Jan 2025) → 50th (spring 2025) → 102 (30 Jan 2026) → 113 (Part 1 August 2026 report, by ward). These are cumulative padlocks since 15 Jul 2024, not a monthly count. A secondary July 2026 summary citing 136,882 patients / 65 retailers vs Part 1's August 143,556 / 66 retailers reflects different months and different metrics (see cross-run conflicts, N-02) — not a contradiction on closures.

12.2 THC driving limits and impairment testing

DC has NO per-se THC blood limit in the sources opened in Run 2. The OAG's official position (opened, 24 October 2026) is impairment-based:

“It remains illegal to operate or to be in physical control of any vehicle while intoxicated or while under the influence of drugs, alcohol, or a combination of the two. See DC Code §50-2206.11. … One is under the influence of a drug when one's ability to operate a motor vehicle is impaired in [a] way that can be perceived or noticed. See Criminal Jury Instruction for the District of Columbia 6.400.”

“Although possession of certain quantities of marijuana has been legalized or decriminalized, it is illegal to smoke or otherwise consume marijuana in a public space or in a vehicle. The [OAG] prosecutes public consumption violations and DUIs.”

oag.dc.gov — Marijuana and Impaired Driving
Conflict flagged VERIFY: A secondary commercial site claims DC's threshold is “5 nanograms of THC per milliliter of blood” with DUI penalties of 180 days / $1,000 (first) and 12 months / $2,500–$5,000 (second). A second secondary site on the same domain correctly states DC “does not have a per se limit for drug metabolites” and prosecutors must prove actual impairment. The OAG impairment standard is the authoritative position; the [Removed — no per se THC driving limit; impairment-based only] claim is not adopted here. Before advising on a numeric limit, open D.C. Code §§50-2206.01, 50-2206.11, 50-2206.13 and the current Criminal Jury Instructions — Gap C-41. Note the [Removed — no per se THC driving limit; impairment-based only] figure is a real per-se limit in other jurisdictions (and in Canada's hybrid offence) and may have been imported in error.
  • Medical patients are not exempt: §7-1671.03(d)(2) — nothing in Chapter 16B permits operating/navigating/being in actual physical control of a motor vehicle, aircraft or motorboat while under the influence of medical cannabis; §7-1671.03(d)(1) likewise bars undertaking any task under the influence where that would constitute negligence/professional malpractice; §7-1671.03(e) — authorised medical use does not create a defence to any crime (except the voluntary-intoxication defence as recognised in DC law).
  • Impairment testing in practice (secondary, consistent with OAG): MPD officers use observations, standardised field sobriety tests and drug-recognition training; blood/breath/urine testing under implied-consent law (refusal → licence revocation for 1 year, secondary claim — verify against §50-2206 provisions, Gap C-41); prosecutors rely on officer testimony plus toxicology, not a THC number alone. No DC roadside oral-fluid per-se screening programme was verified in Run 2.
  • Public consumption in vehicles: Illegal to smoke/consume in a vehicle in a public space (including parked) — see Part 12.4/12.5; OAG page confirms consumption in a vehicle is illegal.

12.3 Expungement and sealing

  • Governing law: Second Chance Amendment Act of 2022, D.C. Law 24-284, codified at D.C. Code §§16-801 et seq. (secondary CCRC/Zuckerman summaries; Code sections not opened line-by-line in Run 2 — Gap C-42 for the current consolidated text and applicability dates, which have been amended by later budget/support and Secure DC legislation).
  • Automatic expungement: Records for offences that have been decriminalised, legalised or held unconstitutional, and records relating to simple possession of marijuana of any quantity before 15 Feb 2015, are subject to automatic expungement. No motion required. The court deadline is stated secondarily as 1 Oct 2027 (or within 90 days after case termination/final disposition, whichever is later) for old records, and within 90 days of termination for new qualifying records. A prosecutor may move to retain a record only on clear and convincing evidence of a lawful purpose (e.g. ongoing investigation).
  • Automatic sealing: For eligible non-convictions and most misdemeanours after a waiting period (secondarily: 10 years after sentence completion for misdemeanour convictions; 17 offence categories excluded from automatic sealing). Petition-based sealing is extended to non-convictions and all misdemeanours and to all but certain violent/sexual felonies, with eased standards/burdens and no requirement to seal all eligible records at once.
  • Expungement by petition: On grounds of actual innocence for non-convictions (more-likely-than-not standard — difficult without counsel, secondary).
  • What is NOT covered: The Act does not automatically expunge all cannabis distribution/sale convictions; relief for those depends on petition eligibility and offence category. Juvenile sealing and human-trafficking victim expungement provisions were not changed by Law 24-284 (CCRC).
  • Practical effect claimed secondarily: Passage of Initiative 71 caused an 88% decrease in marijuana-related arrests in a year (FindLaw summary — secondary, unverified methodology).
Date conflict to flag: Legal Aid DC's 2023 budget analysis notes the FY24 Budget Support Act changed the Second Chance effective date to 1 Jan 2026 and the applicability of the automatic-expungement provision to 1 Oct 2027 in one reading, while CCRC/Zuckerman summaries state the law “took effect 1 Mar 2025” (most provisions) / “2027 for automatic sealing” and a 1 Oct 2027 court deadline. These applicability/effective-date readings conflict in secondary sources. Do not advise a client on a deadline without opening current §§16-802, 16-805 and the latest Budget Support Act applicability section — Gap C-42 is material.

Sources: ccresourcecenter.org — DC Restoration Profile; zuckerman.com — Second Chance Guide; legalclarity.org (all secondary, 24 October 2026).

12.4 Consumption lounges and events

DC does NOT have a general “cannabis lounge” licence for the public. The only lawful on-premises consumption model is the safe-use treatment facility endorsement for a licensed retailer (internet retailers are ineligible).

  • Statute (§7-1671.06c, Code section page): The holder may (A) sell medical cannabis/products/paraphernalia at the retailer to qualifying patients/caregivers to be administered on the premises by/to the patient at the time of purchase within designated consumption areas separated from the remainder by a secure door with a separate ventilation system; (B) allow removal of unused product purchased at the retailer (packaged per §7-1671.13 rules); (C) offer/sell non-cannabis food; (D) offer recorded/background music.
  • Duties: cameras operable/recording ≥30 days; conspicuous warning labels visible to patient/caregiver; destroy abandoned/unclaimed product in the consumption area; package/label on-premises product per §7-1671.13 rules.
  • Area characteristics: isolated from other retailer areas by walls + secure door, access only from the retailer; a smoke-free monitoring area for employees if combustibles are offered; if combustibles are offered, ventilation directing air outside through a pollution/odour-control unit eliminating all detectable odour, smoke and combustion by-products (preventing any public nuisance).
  • Prohibitions: no alcohol/tobacco in the facility; no member of the public other than a qualifying patient/caregiver may enter; no bringing/administering product not purchased at that retailer (unless Board rulemaking permits); no sale/provision above Chapter 16B quantity limits; no organised game/contest involving consuming cannabis or awarding cannabis; no advertising/promoting a prohibited practice on or off premises.
  • Fees (Part 4 baseline, confirmed in Code text): minimum initial application fee $1,000; endorsement valid 3 years, minimum annual fee $2,000 (§7-1671.06c(f)).

Source: code.dccouncil.gov — D.C. Code §7-1671.06c (index, 24 October 2026).

  • Live examples of safe-use applications: Top Level LLC / Aligned, 3715 Macomb St NW, 2nd floor (ANC 3A settlement agreement, Jun 2025: no smoking of burning cannabis/tobacco anywhere on premises; focus on edibles, ≤30 mg THC per customer per treatment session in that settlement); Wash Hydro, 2318 18th St NW (already approved for Delivery + Safe-Use, seeking expansion to 1st floor, Aug 2025 placard; safe-use hours Sun–Sat 11 a.m.–9 p.m.); an applicant at 39 Q St SW (Sep 2025 placard: safe-use + summer garden, ~8,952 sq ft, hours Sun 10 a.m.–10 p.m., Mon–Sat 10 a.m.–11 p.m.); an applicant at 1815 M St NW (Nov 2025 placard: Delivery, Educational Tasting, Safe-Use, Summer Garden). These are applications/placards, not proof of an operating lounge — verify operating status on ABCA's licensed list before visiting.
  • Summer garden endorsement ($300, Part 4 baseline) is an outdoor retailer area endorsement, distinct from safe-use; its conditions were not opened in Run 2 (Gap C-43).
  • Events: There is no DC “cannabis event licence” for public consumption. Events on private property with only lawful gifts/possession are a different question from a commercial event selling cannabis. The H Street Festival enforcement (Part 12.1) shows unlicensed sale at a public festival is shut down as a public nuisance. A licensed retailer may seek event-related substantial changes (e.g. extended hours for Howard Homecoming, ABCA dispositions Oct 2026 — alcohol examples, not cannabis consumption events). Cannabis tourism events marketed as “consumption parties” open to the public, with cannabis sold or gifted as part of admission, should be treated as unlawful commercial activity unless ABCA confirms a specific endorsement covers them.
  • Public consumption generally: Illegal in any public space — streets, sidewalks, alleys, parks, parking areas, any vehicle in a public area, and any place the public is invited (bars, restaurants, stores, private clubs) — D.C. Code §48-911.01 (secondary summaries; Code text not opened in Run 2 — Gap C-44). Secondary penalty summary: misdemeanour, up to 60 days jail (and, in one summary, up to $500 fine). Verify penalty from §48-911.01 before advising.

12.5 Cannabis tourism and visitor rules — including federal land

Visitor purchase rules (lawful route)

  1. Reciprocity: Present a valid, unexpired out-of-state medical registration + government photo ID at a licensed DC retailer (ABCA: confirm your jurisdiction is extended reciprocity first).
  2. Temporary DC registration: Non-residents 21+ may self-certify (no doctor visit required, per secondary ABCA summaries) for 3 days ($10), 30 days ($20), 90 days ($50), 180 days ($75) or 365 days ($100); digital registration usable immediately on payment; also available in person at ABCA, 899 North Capitol St NE, Suite 4200B, or by email to ABCA's cannabis-info inbox (secondary retailer summaries — confirm current portal on abca.dc.gov — Medical Cannabis Patients).
  3. International visitors: Secondary retailer summaries claim international travellers may also apply for temporary registration; ABCA's patients page frames non-residents as persons residing/domiciled in another state/territory/foreign country (§7-1671.01(13B) includes “foreign country, or foreign territory” for non-resident cardholders) — but the temporary-registration eligibility for a visitor with no home medical programme should be confirmed on ABCA's current non-resident page, as secondary lists differ on this point (Gap C-21).
  4. What visitors may NOT do: Buy from a gifting shop, buy from any unlicensed seller, consume in public, consume on federal land, drive impaired, or take DC cannabis home across a state line (Part 10.2). A visitor's DC registration does not authorise possession in their home state.

Federal land — where DC law stops

  • Rule: On federal property in DC, federal law applies, not DC law. Possession/use of any amount of marijuana on federal land is a federal offence, regardless of a DC registration, Initiative 71 possession, or medical status (subject only to the federal Schedule III medical question for state-licensed medical marijuana after 28 Apr 2026 — see below).
  • Places (secondary, consistent across sources): National Mall, West and East Potomac Parks, Rock Creek Park, Anacostia Park, Capitol grounds, Smithsonian properties/museums, federal buildings and their grounds, military installations (Joint Base Anacostia–Bolling), and the many small triangle parks/traffic circles under NPS jurisdiction. Secondary estimates: federal land ≈ 29% of DC, ~18 sq mi. The Metro system is also described secondarily as federal-property-adjacent for enforcement purposes — verify: Metro is operated by WMATA (interstate compact), not NPS; do not state “Metro is federal land” without a source (Gap C-45).
  • Enforcers: U.S. Park Police (NPS), U.S. Capitol Police, Smithsonian security/federal officers, military police on installations.
  • Federal penalties (secondary summaries of 21 U.S.C. §844): Simple possession, first offence: up to 1 year jail + mandatory minimum $1,000 fine; second: 15 days mandatory minimum to 2 years + $2,500 fine; subsequent: up to 3 years + 90-day mandatory minimum (felony in some summaries). NPS-regulation simple possession on parkland is separately summarised as a misdemeanour up to 6 months / $5,000. These are secondary summaries — verify against 21 U.S.C. §844 and 36 CFR Part 1 / NPS regulations before advising (Gap C-45).
  • Tourism advice in one line: Leave cannabis at the hotel (private residence / lawful private place) before visiting the Mall, monuments, museums, parks or Capitol grounds; do not carry it “just in case” across the federal boundary, which is often unmarked (a city sidewalk becoming a park path).
Schedule III nuance VERIFY: After the 28 Apr 2026 federal final rule, state-licensed medical marijuana is Schedule III federally. Whether that changes simple-possession enforcement for a DC-registered patient on federal land (e.g. is DC-registered medical possession now analogous to a Schedule III prescription possession?) is not answered by any DC or NPS source found in Run 2. Do not advise patients that a DC card now protects them on the Mall. NPS/DOJ guidance on this point is Gap C-46 — material and urgent for visitor advice.

12.6 Landlord / tenant rules — including public housing

Private rentals

  • Landlords may ban use and growing on their property, even for medical patients, by lease rule. Initiative 71 and Chapter 16B do not require a landlord to permit smoking or cultivation. Secondary summaries agree: a landlord may enforce non-smoking building rules; if use damages property or disturbs other tenants, that may be grounds for eviction proceedings under general tenancy law.
  • Medical-use administration places (§7-1671.03(b)) are expressly “if permitted”: a patient's residence “if permitted,” another person's residence “if [that] individual has given permission,” a medical treatment facility “if permitted by the facility.” A lease ban therefore removes the “if permitted” condition for that residence.
  • Drug-haven / nuisance eviction (D.C. Code §42-3602): A housing provider (or the Mayor, U.S. Attorney, or local civic/community association for nuisance abatement) may bring an action to recover possession / evict if the court finds by a preponderance that the unit is a drug haven or a nuisance exists, considering (among other factors) whether a tenant/occupant was charged with a Uniform Controlled Substances Act / CSA violation for activity in the housing accommodation, repeat drug-related searches/seizures with arrests, firearm discharge, and witness testimony about possession/manufacture/storage/distribution/use of an illegal drug in the accommodation. Source: code.dccouncil.gov — D.C. Code §42-3602 (index, 24 October 2026). This is a general drug-haven statute, not a cannabis-specific eviction rule — lawful medical use alone should not be equated with “drug haven” without a court finding, and advice should be counsel-led.
  • Unlicensed-establishment landlords (commercial): See Part 12.1 — commercial property owners face Board fines and remediation-plan requirements if they fail to prevent unlawful cannabis retail on their property. Residential landlords are a different category; do not conflate the two fine regimes.
  • Human-rights / disability note (secondary, unverified): One secondary summary claims the DC Human Rights Act bars refusing to rent solely because a person uses medical cannabis. That claim was not verified against the Human Rights Act text or case law in Run 2 and conflicts in practice with the landlord-ban rule above (a ban on use on the premises is different from a refusal to rent because of status). Gap C-47: check D.C. Code Title 2, Chapter 14 and any OHR guidance on medical cannabis and housing.

Public / federally assisted housing — federal rule overrides DC law

  • Rule: Residents of public housing, Section 8 / Housing Choice Voucher and other federally assisted housing can be denied admission or evicted for cannabis use, including medical use, even inside their own apartment and even with a valid DC registration. HUD's position (2011 memorandum, cited secondarily): federal and state nondiscrimination laws do not require PHAs/owners to accommodate medical cannabis; the Fair Housing Act, ADA and Rehabilitation Act expressly exclude illegal drug use (federally illegal use) from reasonable-accommodation duties.
  • Statutory basis (secondary): Federal law allows PHAs/owners to deny admission to a person determined to be using a controlled substance and to terminate tenancy where a household member's use (including state-legal medical use) occurs on the premises or interferes with other residents' peaceful enjoyment; owners in assisted programmes cannot adopt lease provisions affirmatively permitting marijuana use.
  • Pending fix (not law): The Marijuana in Federally Assisted Housing Parity Act (Rep. Eleanor Holmes Norton / Sen. Cory Booker, repeatedly reintroduced, most recently Dec 2025 per NORML) would bar eviction/denial for cannabis use lawful under state/DC law in federally assisted housing. It has not been enacted as of Run 2. Source: norml.org (secondary, 24 October 2026)
  • Schedule III nuance VERIFY: Secondary analysis (mid-2026) states HUD had not updated its guidance in response to medical rescheduling as of mid-2026. Whether Schedule III medical status changes HUD's “illegal drug” analysis for DC-registered patients after 28 Apr 2026 is Gap C-48 — do not advise public-housing patients that rescheduling protects them.

12.7 Gun ownership conflicts

  • Historical federal rule (still the starting point): Under 18 U.S.C. §922(g)(3), a person who is an “unlawful user of or addicted to any controlled substance” is a prohibited person for firearms purchase/possession. ATF Form 4473, Q.21(f), asks whether the buyer is an unlawful user of, or addicted to, marijuana or any other controlled substance, with a warning that marijuana use remains unlawful federally regardless of state legalisation/decriminalisation. Answering “yes” blocks the dealer sale; answering “no” falsely is a separate federal felony (18 U.S.C. §§922(a)(6), 924 — secondary penalty summaries differ: up to 5 years for the false statement vs up to 10 years for prohibited-person possession; verify against current §§922/924, Gap C-49).
  • ATF 2011 open letter: Dealers who know or have reasonable cause to believe a buyer holds a medical marijuana card should treat that person as prohibited and refuse the sale. Upheld in Wilson v. Lynch, 9th Cir. 2016 (card as reasonable cause; Second Amendment burden held not severe enough to invalidate).
  • 2026 developments — DRAFT / EVOLVING, not settled law VERIFY:
    • ATF posted a draft revised Form 4473 (May 2026, per NORML) revising the “unlawful user” definition to acknowledge that the prohibition no longer applies to those who exclusively consume state-authorised medical cannabis (now Schedule III). The draft is not yet official; other form changes require public comment.
    • A DOJ rights-restoration rule (Aug 2026, per Marijuana Moment) is described as creating a process for persons prohibited on drug-use grounds to regain rights by abstaining for 12 months (plus other conditions), and as excluding marijuana from the “controlled substance” definition in a related filing excerpt. Its final text, effective date and application to DC medical patients vs adult-use users were not verified from the Federal Register in Run 2.
    • Litigation (U.S. v. Hemani and related Second Amendment challenges) is described secondarily as prompting ATF to issue guidance “soon.”
  • DC-specific advice position for Run 2: (1) Adult-use (non-medical) cannabis users remain “unlawful users” federally under the baseline (adult-use = Schedule I) — the gun prohibition plainly still applies to them. (2) DC-registered medical patients are in a transitional, unverified position after 28 Apr 2026: the Schedule III split logically undermines the “unlawful user” premise for exclusively state-licensed medical use, and ATF's draft points that way, but no final ATF rule, final Form 4473 or DC-specific guidance was located to state that medical patients may now lawfully purchase/possess firearms. (3) No DC law creates a DC firearms right that overrides federal §922. Gap C-49 is material: check the Federal Register for the final Form 4473, the DOJ rights-restoration rule, and any ATF guidance before advising any DC patient on firearms.

Sources (all secondary, 24 October 2026): norml.org — ATF draft Form 4473; Marijuana Moment — DOJ rule; legalclarity.org

12.8 Pet / veterinary products

  • No DC veterinary cannabis product rule was found in Run 2. There is no DC “pet CBD” licence, no DC animal-cannabis product standard, and no DC veterinary-board rule on cannabis/CBD discussion located on DC government pages searched.
  • What applies by default in DC: (a) A cannabinoid product marketed for animals is still a “cannabis product” under DC's no-exemption definition if it contains cannabis-derived cannabinoids — it cannot be sold as an unlicensed “hemp pet treat” outside the medical programme on DC's own logic (Part 7), though enforcement practice for pet products specifically was not found. (b) Federally, FDA has not approved CBD for animal use; AVMA guidance (secondary, 2019) states U.S. vets may not prescribe/dispense/recommend cannabis for animals — that is a professional-guidance summary, not DC law. (c) Neighbouring-state contrast (not DC law): Maryland enacted a law effective 1 Oct 2026 barring its veterinary board from disciplining vets solely for discussing/recommending cannabis/CBD for animals for potential therapeutic/health-supplementation purposes (HB 452, signed 14 Apr 2026 — secondary, dvm360). California adopted animal cannabis product standards effective 1 Jan 2026 (≤1 mg total THC per package, edible/orally consumed concentrate/topical only). Neither applies in DC; they are included only to show DC has no equivalent rule.
  • Gap C-50: Check DC Board of Veterinary Medicine / DLCP veterinary rules and FDA animal-CBD guidance before advising a DC pet-product business or a DC vet. Do not advise pet owners to give a pet a human medical-cannabis product — THC toxicity risk in animals is a veterinary-medical question, not a licensing question.

12.9 Medical cannabis in schools

  • Statute (§7-1671.03(b)(5), Code text): Medical cannabis may only be administered by/to a qualifying patient at (1) the patient's residence if permitted, (2) another person's residence with permission, (3) a medical treatment facility when receiving care (if permitted), (4) a licensed safe-use treatment facility, or (5) a school in which the qualifying patient is enrolled, if the school has a policy in place for allowing the administration of medication at school; provided, that the medical cannabis shall be in non-smokable form.
  • Student Access to Treatment Amendment Act of 2020, D.C. Law 23-81 (amending §38-651.03): If a student is a qualifying patient and failure to administer medical marijuana during the school day would disrupt the student's ability to participate in instruction, a medication action plan may include administration of medical marijuana in non-smokable form. The plan must include an authorised-practitioner certification to that effect. A school shall adopt policies permitting a qualifying-patient student to administer medical marijuana on campus during the school day as necessary under the medical authorisation. A school may discontinue compliance if, after 7 Oct 2019, the federal government communicates that federal funding will be withheld from DC/a DC school if it continues to authorise on-campus administration.
  • Practice (secondary, 2019): DC Public Schools issued a policy update acknowledging administrators are authorised to provide non-smoked medical cannabis products to authorised patients on school grounds (NORML, Sep 2019). Whether every DCPS school / charter school currently has a compliant medication-action-plan process, and who administers (nurse vs caregiver vs self-administration), was not verified from a current DCPS policy in Run 2 — Gap C-51: check DCPS / OSSE medication-administration policy and the current §38-651.03 text.
  • Limits: Smokable/vaped product is not permitted at school under this rule (non-smokable only). The school rule does not authorise possession/use by non-patients, adult-use use on school grounds, or use at a school that has no medication policy. Federal funding / Drug-Free Schools considerations are the stated reason a school may discontinue the policy (Law 23-81, above).

Sources: law.justia.com — §7-1671.03; preview.code.dccouncil.gov — §7-1671.03; preview.code.dccouncil.gov — D.C. Law 23-81 (index, 24 October 2026).

  1. C-02 Hemp registry: No registry found (ABCA/USDA/search). Follow-up: ABCA medical cannabis business pages + USDA HeMP public search tool (ams.usda.gov — hemp FAQ — “Hemp Public Search Tool” reference).

  3. C-04 OAG AL-21-222: Memorandum not opened in Run 2. Follow-up: oag.dc.gov — opinions / AL-21-222 PDF (28 Apr 2021).
  4. C-05 Capitol Hemp litigation: Cited only in secondary analysis; docket, holding and current status not verified. Follow-up: D.D.C. docket search for Capitol Hemp, LLC.
  5. C-06 Hemp transit through DC: 2018 Farm Bill through-shipment bar vs DC enforcement practice for hemp in transit — no DC source found. Follow-up: ABCA/MPD guidance; 7 U.S.C. §1639o note (through-shipment provision).
  6. C-07 Delta-8 schedule listing: Secondary claim that DC schedule names delta-8 specifically — not verified in §48-902.04 text. Follow-up: code.dccouncil.gov — Title 48, Chapter 9, Subchapter II (schedules).
  7. C-08 Cannabis-zoned parcels: No published DC cannabis-parcel map/list found; ABCA GIS maps are application-issued. Follow-up: DOB zoning map / Zoning Information System + ABCA licensed-premises list for any specific address; broker detail page for 1840 Fenwick St NE on 420property.com (detail listing, not index).
